> The right software can streamline workflows and employee collaboration. Active network security protects a business’s most valuable assets. And managing hardware forms the backbone of these initiatives.

> Considering this scope, IT leaders develop and advocate for their organization’s strategy, often with data shaping their decisions, presentations and day-to-day responsibilities.

> Warrington’s degree explores information technology in this multilayered context. The program:
> 
> Instructs you in the technical skills for designing, developing and implementing an organization’s IT strategy, including programming, networks and information systems.
> Introduces data — including management, databases, analysis, statistical modeling and visualization — as a problem-solving tool.
> Illustrates how IT, information systems and data support and enrich core business functions and decisions.
> Incorporates group projects throughout that replicate the workplace and challenge you to devise a solution with all you’ve acquired.
> Encourages you to grow your resume with a summer internship or business practicum project.

> Fast-track your aspirations in a hands-on program that trains you for an in-demand career and keeps debt down.

> Along with completing your FAFSA® to qualify for loans and other types of aid, you may be eligible for scholarships or undergraduate tuition rates based on how you apply:
> 
> Scholarships: The MS in Information Systems and Operations Management program offers select scholarship and research assistant opportunities to students who have attended the program for at least a semester.
> Florida Bright Futures: Are you surging ahead with the combination track? Florida Bright Futures recipients who graduated with their bachelor’s degree in seven semesters/105 credits or less may be eligible to apply remaining Florida Bright Futures funding to the first semester of the MS in Information Systems and Operations Management program.
> Florida Prepaid Students: Once accepted into the combination program, Florida Prepaid students may apply any remaining prepaid funds toward graduate tuition.
> Explore all funding options
> Formats for the MS-ISOM, IT concentration
> There’s no single path to beginning a career in IT and information systems. Individuals who majored in business or technology to the humanities and the arts enroll in the MS-ISOM program through one of the following formats:
> 
> Traditional on-campus program: Whether you’re debugging devices or making a case for a new system, you’ll collaborate with other IT and business professionals in your future job. This format gives you a glimpse of these responsibilities through intensive group learning and relationship-building opportunities.
> Combination program: When you envision yourself managing networks and data, realize your aspirations on your timeframe with this program that allows you to begin master’s coursework as an undergraduate and earn two credentials in less time. Learn more about our combination degrees.
> MBA/MS-ISOM program: Show up well-rounded in business and technology fundamentals by pairing Warrington’s Full-Time MBA with MS-ISOM courses. Explore our joint MBA programs.
> Auditing Minor: Financial audits take into account how a company stores, manages, and accesses its assets. In this minor, you’ll examine how technology supports the auditing process, from various risks to tools and practices.
> Please note that candidates without a business background may spend an additional semester in the program.
> 
> How to apply to the MS-ISOM program
> Bachelor’s degree or a four-year international equivalent.
> Students without an undergraduate business major or minor will need to complete core business courses. It is also recommended that students take a programming course.
> 3.0 upper-division undergraduate GPA (U.S. degrees only).
> A statement of purpose (400-600 words) that explains how the MS in Information Systems and Operations Management aligns with your future goals.
> Résumé or Curriculum Vitae covering all post-high school education and employment, international affiliations, and research funding.
> Two or more letters of recommendation from a supervisor. Recommendations from student/peer leaders will not be accepted.
> Official or unofficial transcripts from all undergraduate and graduate institutions attended. Send electronically or mailed in a sealed, signed, and unopened envelope to Graduate Admissions.
> The program does not require a GRE or GMAT score. Test scores may enhance your application, which is reviewed in our holistic review process. If you decide to submit scores use code: UF 5812.
> $30 application fee.
> International candidates: Refer to requirements for international students, including English proficiency and transcript evaluations. Warrington interviews candidates who attended a non-U.S. institution as part of the admissions process.

> The UF Office of Admissions will not recognize 3-year bachelor’s degrees from India, Australia, Bangladesh, Canada (excluding Quebec), Nepal and Pakistan. Three-year bachelor’s degrees from other countries are reviewed on an individual basis.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 19:09

I'll dig into our past conversations about GSDSI first, then come back with questions.

> *Claude used conversation_search: GSDSI internship essay*

Good context. Let me pull a bit more detail on the internship specifics.

> *Claude used conversation_search: internship reflection overall reflection changed career path Daniel Kaplan*

Let me research the UF programs and organizations you mentioned.

> *Claude used web_search: Heavener Leadership Challenge UF Warrington cohort*

> *Claude used web_search: UF Accent Speakers Bureau assistant comptroller student government*

> *Claude used web_search: UF Warrington combination degree MS-ISOM Data Science concentration requirements*

I read through our past chats — the internship prep gameplan from March, the goal-setting essay (the tools hub, expense tracker, presale analyzer, client intake form, ops dashboard), and the end-of-internship Career Readiness Reflection with Daniel Kaplan's evaluation. I also looked up HLC, ACCENT, and the combination degree structure. Before I write anything, I need to resolve one structural issue and mine you for detail.

**The structural issue first:** you attached the **Information Technology** concentration page, but you wrote that the internship made you want this degree "with its emphasis on data science." Those are two different concentrations with two different admissions pages. This isn't a small thing — the last paragraph of the essay has to name the concentration and tie your goals to *its* curriculum, and a reader in the IT track will notice if the essay is really arguing for Data Science.

Also worth knowing: 400–600 words is *short*. That's roughly four paragraphs. A story-driven essay can absolutely work here, but it means **one scene, not three**. We pick the single sharpest moment, land it fast, and spend the rest proving the claim. So my questions are aimed at finding which moment is strongest.

---

## A. Decisions I need from you

1. **Which concentration are you applying to — Information Technology or Data Science?** (Or are you undecided and want the essay to work for the one you name?)
2. **Combination program or traditional?** Your resume says BSBA May 2029. The combination track lets you double-count up to 16 credits and start master's coursework as an undergrad. Which are you applying through, and **what term are you applying to start** (Fall 2027? Fall 2028?)
3. **Is this the same essay for the UF application portal**, or are you also emailing it? (Affects nothing in content, just wanted to confirm it's the 400–600 word SOP and not a different prompt.)

## B. The AP Computer Science turning point — this is your opening scene

Right now this is an idea, not a scene. I need the picture.

4. **What exactly were you doing when it clicked?** Were you stuck on a specific assignment or project? Debugging something at 1am? Sitting in class? Give me the actual moment — the more specific and small, the better it works.
5. **Before AP CS, what did you actually think you were going to do?** Did you plan to apply as a Computer Science major? Had you told people that? Was there a version of you that had already committed to it out loud?
6. **What was the thing you couldn't do — and what was the thing you realized you *could* do?** The essay is much stronger if the realization isn't "coding is hard" but something like "I was better at figuring out *what to build* than at building it." What's the true version of that for you?
7. **Where did you read the definition of information systems that made it click?** UF's site? Somewhere else? Do you remember roughly what it said? (You mentioned "bridge between business and technology.") Was this before or after you switched at Preview?
8. **What actually happened at UF Preview?** Did you change your major on the spot? Talk to an advisor? That's a real, datable moment and it could be a great pivot line.

## C. GSDSI — this is your proof section

I have a lot from past chats, but I need what only you know.

9. **Of everything you built** (expense tracker, ops dashboard, presale analyzer, client data intake form, tools hub), **which one is the best story?** I'd guess the tools hub or the presale analyzer, but you know which one people actually use.
10. **What did the team do *before* that tool existed?** Spreadsheets? A Slack thread? Someone doing it by hand for two hours? This before/after contrast is the single most persuasive thing you can put in the essay.
11. **Is there a moment where someone reacted?** Daniel saying you were exceeding expectations, someone on the team saying "wait, you built this?", leadership asking for access. One quoted or paraphrased human reaction beats three paragraphs of description.
12. **Was there a moment where something failed or you got it wrong first?** An essay where everything works is less believable and less interesting. Did you build something nobody used? Misunderstand what a team needed?
13. **The "bridged business and technology" claim — what's the single clearest example?** Where were you specifically translating between a business need and a technical build?
14. **You're returning as Director of Interns this fall.** How did that happen — did you ask, or did they offer? What will you be responsible for?

## D. The future

15. **What do you actually want to be doing five years out?** Past chats say business analytics in South Florida. Still accurate, or has the internship moved it?
16. **Is there anything specific in the UF curriculum you want** — a course, the practicum, the summer internship, AI/machine learning integration, a faculty member? Naming one real thing signals you did homework.
17. **Why the master's at all, rather than just going to work after your BSBA?** This is the question the committee is quietly asking. What does the degree give you that four years and a good internship don't?

## E. The prompt's hidden ask

The prompt says: *highlight what we cannot assess from the other application materials.* Your resume already shows GPA, leadership, and the internship. So:

18. **What's the thing about you that a reader would never guess from that resume?** Could be about how you learn, why you take on so much, something about your family, why men's mental health advocacy matters to you, something else entirely.
19. **You do an unusual amount** — Senate, Homecoming web design, Thrive IFC, HLC, Vision Party, ACCENT, GSDSI. **Is there a through-line you'd name yourself?** I have a theory (you keep ending up as the person who builds or fixes the system other people are using — the Homecoming site, the tutoring program you founded, the tools hub, Senate budget process), but I'd rather hear yours than impose mine.
20. **Which of the new roles do you actually want in the essay?** HLC, Vision Party Director of Ambassadors, ACCENT Assistant Comptroller, GSDSI Director of Interns. At 400–600 words we can name maybe one or two, and only if they earn it. Also: **should I be updating your resume too**, or is the resume final and this is essay-only?

---

Answer in whatever order you want, shorthand is fine, and skip anything that doesn't have a real answer — I'd rather have eight honest answers than twenty invented ones. Question **1** is the one I can't move forward without.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 19:27

Let me verify a few things before I write — the curriculum, the combination timeline, and the tools you used.

> *Claude used web_fetch: https://warrington.ufl.edu/master-of-science-in-information-systems-and-operations-management/courses-and-curriculum/*

Now the combination degree requirements — this matters for your Spring 2027 timeline.

> *Claude used web_fetch: https://warrington.ufl.edu/undergraduate/combination-degrees/*

Research done. Three things I found that change the essay, then my remaining questions.

## Things you need to know before we write

**1. Your deadline is September 1 — that's ten days out.** The MS-ISOM combination degree Spring deadline for undergraduate students is September 1. If you're targeting Spring 2027, we need this finished this week, not next month.

**2. MAN5502 is the wrong course to name — and naming it would hurt you.** I checked the combination degree course tables. MAN5502 Production & Operations Management is the SCM Track substitution — it's the *Supply Chain* option. For the Data Science track, the parallel course is ISM6251, DS Track: Advanced Business Programming. Putting a supply chain course in a Data Science statement of purpose is exactly the kind of detail an admissions reader notices. Real Data Science courses you could name instead, all from the actual curriculum: Data Mining for Business Intelligence, Business Intelligence, Business Data Presentation and Visualization, and Programming for Business Analytics (Python). There's also the business analytics practicum, where you're placed with a Warrington partner company to solve a real problem and present the solution — that one maps almost perfectly onto what you already did at GSDSI, and it's the strongest thing you could name.

**3. Good news on the credits.** Because you're an Information Systems major, you can take 16 credits of graduate coursework during junior and senior years, versus 12 for non-IS majors. Eligibility is a 3.2 cumulative UF GPA, 60 completed credit hours (AP, IB, AICE and dual enrollment count), and all Universal Tracking courses finished with grades posted. Your 3.8 clears the GPA easily. Also worth knowing they call it an "honors" path, more selective, where meeting the minimums doesn't guarantee admission — so the essay is doing real work here.

---

## On question 17 — I'm not going to make something up

You asked me to invent a reason you want the master's. I won't do that, and not to be difficult about it: a fabricated motive is the easiest thing in the world for an admissions reader to spot, and it's the one paragraph where they're actually evaluating you rather than your resume. It also puts your name on a claim you'd have to defend if they follow up.

But I don't think you need to invent anything, because your answer to #18 already contains it. You told me you learned by jumping in — no experience with any of it, used AI to figure it out, got good enough to teach others. That's genuinely impressive, and it's also exactly where the limit is. You can build a system that sends a campaign in minutes. What you can't yet do is tell anyone *why* one campaign outperformed another, or model it, or prove it. You automated the work; you didn't analyze the result. That's the gap a Data Science concentration fills, and it's true rather than invented.

**Is that right?** If yes, that's your answer to "why the master's," and it's a strong one. If it's not how you'd put it, tell me what's off.

---

## Remaining questions

1. **Do you have 60 credit hours yet?** You'd be starting your second year this fall. With AP/dual enrollment from Palmetto you might already be there, but I don't want to assume. Have you talked to a Heavener combination degree advisor?
2. **Have you actually submitted the application form yet?** The essay gets emailed to msisom@warrington.ufl.edu, but the online form comes first.
3. **The email campaign tool — what happened to it?** Is it still running? Roughly how many campaigns has it sent? "A day down to a few minutes" is your best line, and one number behind it makes it land.
4. **Did you build it because someone asked, or did you notice the problem yourself?** This matters a lot. If you saw the bottleneck and proposed the fix, that's the whole essay.
5. **The n8n errors — was there one that took you the longest?** You don't need a dramatic failure, just one honest detail. The unused ops dashboard is also usable, and I like that you diagnosed *why* it went unused.
6. **HLC — which term, and were you a participant?** It's a 10-week program, 65 business students selected, built on Kouzes and Posner's Student Leadership Challenge. Confirming you were a selected participant matters, since selectivity is the point.
7. **Vision Party Director of Ambassadors — what do you actually do?** I know Vision is a UF student government party, but I'd rather you tell me the role than have me guess at it.
8. **Your supervisor Daniel — you said you want to do what he does. What is that, exactly?** VP of Operations at a data company. Is it his job you want, or something about how he works?
9. **Last one, and it's the real one: what's the honest answer to why Spring 2027 instead of waiting?** You'd be starting graduate coursework unusually early. A reader will wonder. Is it Bright Futures/Florida Prepaid, momentum from the internship, wanting to be back at GSDSI or somewhere similar sooner? Whatever the true reason is, saying it plainly is better than leaving them to guess.

Answer these and I'll write the draft. Given the September 1 deadline, I'd start the online form today regardless of where the essay stands.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 19:35

Two questions I'd normally ask, but I'll state my assumptions instead so you can just correct them and we don't lose a day:

- I'm describing the workflow as: n8n pulls and uploads leads into Instantly, builds the campaign, then routes it to Slack for approval before sending. Anthropic API writes the copy. Correct me if I have the order or the role of any piece wrong.
- I dropped the "30+ years of experience" framing, because your own internship paper says GSDSI was founded in 2018. A reader who looks them up would catch that. I used their data catalog scale instead.

Here's the draft. 570 words.

---

When I applied to the University of Florida, I applied as a computer science major. I had always been good with computers, and computer science seemed like the closest thing to what I was already good at. Before I ever started, I spoke with a UF senior who had just accepted a project management position at American Express straight out of college. That was the job I wanted. He was not a computer science major. He was an information systems major, and I had never heard of the degree.

I looked it up that night and found it described as the field that bridges the gap between business needs and technology. I had spent months assuming I needed to become a great programmer to work in technology. What I actually wanted was to decide what got built and why. By the time I arrived at Preview, I had already made up my mind, and I changed my major before taking a single class at UF.

This past summer I worked as a Marketing and Data Operations Apprentice at Global Source Data Solutions, a data-as-a-service company in Fort Lauderdale, under the Vice President of Operations. I did not expect an incoming sophomore to be genuinely useful at a company managing consumer and business datasets across more than 150 countries. What I found was that I was useful in a specific way. I noticed that launching one email campaign took most of a day. Someone had to pull the leads, upload them into Instantly, build the campaign, enter every setting by hand, and then wait on approval from a sales team that was already busy.

Nobody asked me to fix that. I built an automated workflow using n8n, Vercel, Slack, Instantly, and the Anthropic API that routes each campaign to Slack for approval before it sends. A campaign now takes a few minutes instead of a day, and the team runs two or three daily. It did not work the first time. A single unchecked setting would stop the entire process, and early versions wrote copy that sounded confident and was factually wrong, so I rewrote the prompts until the system understood what the company actually sells. I return this fall as Director of Interns, responsible for onboarding and managing the next group.

That summer also showed me where my limits are. I learn by jumping in. I had no experience with any of these tools in May, and by August I could teach them. But I automated the work without being able to analyze the result. I can build a system that sends a campaign in minutes. I cannot yet tell you, with any rigor, why one campaign outperformed another.

That gap is why I want the MS-ISOM with a Data Science concentration. I do not want to leave UF with only a general understanding of how businesses use technology. Courses like Data Mining for Business Intelligence and Business Data Presentation and Visualization, along with the business analytics practicum, would give me the technical foundation behind instincts I have so far been running on. The combination degree lets me start that work now rather than graduating, working for several years, and coming back later.

Long term, I want to run operations at a data company, which is what my supervisor does. Warrington is where I learned that job existed, and it is where I want to become qualified for it.

---

**A few notes on choices I made.** I left out Senate, Homecoming, Thrive, ACCENT, Vision, and HLC entirely. At 570 words there was no room, they're all on your resume, and the prompt asks for what the resume *can't* show. I also cut the "AI means you don't need to learn every detail of coding" argument. It's a real belief, but stated to a program that teaches Java and Python it reads as dismissive of the curriculum. The version above makes the same pivot without the risk.

The line doing the most work is "Nobody asked me to fix that." That's the sentence that separates you from every other applicant with an internship.

### Ben · 2026-08-22 19:37

heres how the email campaign works

> [!note]- Attached: attachment
> Here's the full picture of how the Campaign Factory works, from idea to launched campaign.
> 
> ## The one-sentence version
> 
> You (or Todd, Nash, or Ben) fill out a 2-minute web form describing a campaign; AI drafts the whole email sequence; it posts to Slack for review; Todd or Dan reacts 👍; and it launches in Instantly, with compliance checks, suppression filtering, and sending infrastructure handled automatically in between.
> 
> ## The flow, step by step
> 
> **1. You prepare the leads (the only manual work).** Find your audience in Apollo, export with verified emails, upload the CSV to Instantly as a named list (format: `Audience_Region_Count_Date`). This stays manual by choice, the Apollo web app finds better emails than any automated route, and lead quality is the one place a human eye matters most. Lists stay under 1,000 per campaign (bigger audiences get split).
> 
> **2. You submit a brief.** The form at gsdsi-brief-wizard.lovable.app, gated by personal invite codes (four people have them). It asks: vertical (preset or custom), sender (Todd or Andrew), offer, call to action, number of emails (3 or 4), the Instantly list name (must match exactly), and lead count. Behind the scenes, the form's backend attaches a secret key and sends the brief to the pipeline, no submission without a valid code, no acceptance without the key.
> 
> **3. The Factory drafts.** The brief flows into the automation pipeline (n8n), which validates it, then has Claude write the full email sequence in GSDSI voice: correct footers (sender | title | address | opt-out line), no invented statistics, vertical-appropriate angle. A validator then checks the draft against hard rules, footer format, compliance language, before anything posts. Political-vertical briefs trigger the voter-file compliance rules automatically.
> 
> **4. A review card posts in #campaign-approvals.** Within about a minute: a summary card (campaign, vertical, audience, sender, compliance notes) with the four full emails in the thread. Nothing has sent, the card is a proposal.
> 
> **5. Approve or revise.** Happy? Todd or Dan reacts 👍, only their reactions count; the interns can submit but not launch. Want changes? Anyone replies in the thread in plain English ("shorten email 1", "change the subjects to direct value style"), a revised card posts in about a minute, revisable as many times as needed. **The golden rule: always react to the newest card**, superseded cards go dead.
> 
> **6. Launch happens automatically on the 👍.** The system pulls the lead list from Instantly, strips anyone on the do-not-contact suppression list (150 client emails and domains, plus everyone who's ever opted out), attaches the 48 healthy GSDSI sending inboxes, sets the schedule (Mon-Fri 9-5 Central, emails spaced over ~2 weeks, replies stop the sequence for that person), creates and activates the campaign in Instantly, and replies on the card with the real numbers, how many leads uploaded, how many were skipped.
> 
> **7. Ongoing behavior.** Opt-outs (STOP replies) and bounces get captured and suppressed from all future campaigns. Interested replies get routed to Todd same-day. Every campaign lives as a row in the database with full history, status, revisions, who approved, when it launched.
> 
> ## The safety design
> 
> The system is built to **fail closed**: if anything is wrong, wrong list name, too many suppressed leads, oversized list, the campaign simply doesn't launch and the card gets a ❌ reply explaining exactly why. The worst case is always "nothing happened," never "the wrong thing sent." On top of that: the human gate (no send without a 👍 ever), locked doors (invite codes + secret key), and a kill switch, one database setting pauses every launch across the whole Factory instantly.
> 
> ## The division of labor
> 
> **Humans decide:** who to target, what to offer, and whether each campaign ships. **The Factory does:** everything mechanical, drafting, compliance, suppression, infrastructure, scheduling, record-keeping. AI proposes; people dispose.
> 
> That's the whole machine. Want this written up as a document, or is there a specific part you want to go deeper on?

The bigger find is the fail-closed design. Your answer to my question 5 was "the prompts were making stuff up, so we fixed the prompts." But what you actually did was build a validator and a system that refuses to launch when anything is wrong. That's a different and much more impressive claim — it's engineering judgment, not prompt tinkering. It's now the strongest paragraph in the essay.

Daniel is your obvious first letter — ask him today, since he needs to email it directly to the program himself. Student or peer leaders won't be accepted, so your second letter needs to be a professor, employer, or advisor.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 19:40

Here it is, ready to copy:

---

Benjamin Jones
Statement of Purpose
MS in Information Systems and Operations Management, Data Science Concentration (Combination Degree)

When I applied to the University of Florida, I applied as a computer science major. I had always been good with computers, and computer science seemed like the closest fit. Before I started, I spoke with a UF senior who had just accepted a project management job at American Express straight out of college. That was the job I wanted. He was not a computer science major. He was an information systems major, and I had never heard of the degree.

I looked it up that night and found it described as the field that bridges the gap between business needs and technology. I had spent months assuming that working in technology meant becoming a great programmer. What I actually wanted was to decide what got built and why. By the time I arrived at Preview, I had already made up my mind, and I changed my major before taking a single class at UF.

This past summer I worked as a Marketing and Data Operations Apprentice at Global Source Data Solutions, a data-as-a-service company in Fort Lauderdale. I did not expect an incoming sophomore to be useful at a company managing datasets across more than 150 countries. What I found was that I was useful in a specific way. I noticed that launching a single email campaign took most of a day, and that a busy sales team was the bottleneck on work that was mostly mechanical.

Nobody asked me to fix that. I built what the team now calls the Campaign Factory. Anyone on the team fills out a two-minute form; an automated pipeline drafts the full email sequence in the company's voice, checks it against our compliance rules, and posts it to Slack as a proposal. Nothing sends until a director approves it. On approval, the system filters the audience against our do-not-contact list, attaches the sending infrastructure, schedules the sequence, and launches. A campaign now takes minutes instead of a day, and the team runs two or three daily.

It did not work the first time. Early drafts invented statistics that sounded plausible and were false. The lesson was not that I needed a better prompt. It was that the system had to fail closed: if anything is wrong, the campaign does not launch and tells you why. The worst outcome is that nothing happens, never that the wrong thing sends. I deliberately left lead selection manual, because that is where human judgment actually earns its keep. I return this fall as Director of Interns.

That summer also showed me my limits. I learn by jumping in. I had no experience with any of these tools in May, and by August I could teach them. But I automated the work without being able to analyze the result. I can build a system that sends a campaign in minutes. I cannot yet tell you, with rigor, why one campaign outperformed another.

That gap is why I want the MS-ISOM with a Data Science concentration. I do not want to leave UF with only a general understanding of how businesses use technology. Courses like Data Mining for Business Intelligence and Business Data Presentation and Visualization, along with the business analytics practicum, would give me the technical foundation behind instincts I have so far been running on. The combination degree lets me start that work now rather than graduating, working several years, and coming back later.

Long term, I want to run operations at a data company, which is what my supervisor does. Warrington is where I learned that job existed, and where I want to become qualified for it.

---

601 words. Set it to 12pt, single or 1.15 spacing, and it'll run just over a page.

Benjamin Jones

When I applied to the University of Florida, I applied as a computer science major. I had always been good with computers, and it seemed like the closest fit. Before I started, I spoke with a UF senior who had just accepted a project management job at American Express. That was the job I wanted, and he was not a computer science major. But I automated the work without being able to analyze it. I can build a system that sends a campaign in minutes. I cannot yet tell you, with rigor, why one campaign outperformed another.

That gap is why I want the MS-ISOM with a Data Science concentration. I do not want to leave UF with only a general understanding of how businesses use technology. Courses like Data Mining for Business Intelligence and Business Data Presentation and Visualization, along with the business analytics practicum, would give me the technical foundation behind instincts I have been running on. The combination degree lets me start that work now rather than graduating, working several years, and coming back later.

Long term, I want to run operations at a data company, which is what my supervisor does. Warrington is where I learned that job existed, and where I want to become qualified for it.

---

**What changed:** the Factory is now one paragraph of mechanics instead of two, and I cut the "two or three daily" figure and the sending-infrastructure details. The freed words went into a new fifth paragraph that widens the frame — the tutoring program, the Homecoming sites, and the dashboard nobody used. That last one is doing real work: admitting you built something well that solved a nonexistent problem is more convincing than another success, and it sets up the "I can build but not yet analyze" admission that follows.

The Factory is still your centerpiece, which it should be. It's just now evidence of a pattern rather than the whole case.

621 is over the stated 600, but "ideal length" is guidance and no reader counts. If you'd rather be safely inside it, the tutoring sentence is the easiest cut.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 19:48

Let me check what the actual certificate is before advising — I don't want to have you name it wrong.

> *Claude used web_search: University of Florida AI certificate undergraduate Artificial Intelligence Fundamentals and Applications*

I looked it up. It's the AI Fundamentals and Applications Certificate — a 9-credit university-wide certificate open to all majors, consisting of a fundamentals course, an ethics course, and a college-specific application course. The two required courses are EEL 3872 Fundamentals of Artificial Intelligence and PHI 3861 Ethics, Data and Technology, it's pitched at junior level with MAC 1147 or equivalent as a prerequisite, and you have to apply through UF Admissions before taking your second course and then apply to graduate with it.

**My honest recommendation: leave it out of the essay, with one possible exception.**

The reason is structural. Your essay makes a clean argument — I'm strong on AI tools, I'm weak on analytical rigor, the Data Science concentration fixes the weak part. Adding "I also want an AI certificate" pushes toward the thing you're *already* strongest at, which softens the gap you just admitted. It can also read as credential collecting, which is the one impression you don't want when you're applying to start grad coursework early. A reader who's already thinking "this kid is doing a lot" doesn't need another item.

**The exception is the ethics course.** Right now your essay says nothing about data ethics, and that's a genuine hole for someone whose stated goal is running operations at a data company. GSDSI's whole positioning is provenance and consent documentation on every dataset — you wrote about that in your internship reflection. And PHI 3861 connects directly to your fail-closed paragraph, because refusing to send when something's wrong *is* an ethics decision expressed as engineering.

If you want it in, here's the version I'd use. Replace the last sentence of the "That gap is why" paragraph with:

> I am also pursuing UF's certificate in AI Fundamentals and Applications, in part for its coursework in data ethics, which matters in an industry built on other people's information. The combination degree lets me start that work now rather than graduating, working several years, and coming back later.

That's +26 words, putting you at 647. You'd need to cut the tutoring sentence to stay reasonable.

**Which I'd only do if you're actually doing it.** "I am pursuing" implies you've applied or are enrolled. If it's still a maybe, say "I plan to pursue" — or leave it out, because a vague future intention is the weakest kind of sentence in a statement of purpose.

Separately from the essay: the application timing is a real thing to handle. You have to apply through UF Admissions before you take your second certificate course, so if you're planning EEL 3872 or PHI 3861 soon, get the certificate application in first or you'll have to redo the sequence.

Exactly 600. Here it is:

Benjamin Jones
Statement of Purpose
MS in Information Systems and Operations Management, Data Science Concentration (Combination Degree)

I applied to the University of Florida as a computer science major. I had always been good with computers, and it seemed like the closest fit. Before I started, I spoke with a UF senior who had just accepted a project management job at American Express. That was the job I wanted, and he was not a computer science major. He was studying information systems, a degree I had never heard of.

I looked it up that night and found it described as the field that bridges the gap between business needs and technology. I had spent months assuming that working in technology meant becoming a great programmer. What I actually wanted was to decide what got built and why. I changed my major at Preview, before taking a class at UF.

This past summer I worked as a Marketing and Data Operations Apprentice at Global Source Data Solutions, a data-as-a-service company managing datasets across 150 countries. I did not expect an incoming sophomore to be useful there. Then I noticed that launching one email campaign took most of a day, and that a busy sales team was the bottleneck on work that was mostly mechanical. Nobody asked me to fix that. I built what the team now calls the Campaign Factory: anyone fills out a two-minute form, an automated pipeline drafts the sequence in the company's voice and checks it against our compliance rules, and it posts to Slack as a proposal that cannot send without a director's approval. A campaign now takes minutes instead of a day.

It did not work at first. Early drafts invented statistics that sounded plausible and were false. The fix was not a better prompt but a system designed to fail closed. The worst outcome is that nothing happens, never that the wrong thing sends. I also left lead selection manual on purpose, because that is where human judgment earns its keep.

That instinct is not new. At UF I manage the Homecoming and Gator Growl websites. At GSDSI I also built an operations dashboard nobody used, and I learned as much from that as from the Factory: it worked exactly as designed and solved a problem the company did not have. What I keep returning to is finding what is not working and building something that leaves an organization more organized than I found it. I return to GSDSI this fall as Director of Interns.

The summer also showed me my limits. I learn by jumping in; I had no experience with any of these tools in May, and by August I could teach them. But I can build a system that sends a campaign in minutes and cannot yet tell you, with rigor, why one outperformed another.

That gap is why I want the MS-ISOM with a Data Science concentration. I do not want to leave UF with only a general understanding of how businesses use technology. Courses like Data Mining for Business Intelligence and Business Data Presentation and Visualization, along with the business analytics practicum, would give me the foundation behind instincts I have been running on. I also plan to pursue UF's certificate in AI Fundamentals and Applications, in part for its coursework in data ethics, which matters in an industry built on other people's information. The combination degree lets me start that work now rather than graduating, working several years, and coming back later.

Long term, I want to run operations at a data company, which is what my supervisor does. Warrington is where I learned that job existed, and where I want to become qualified for it.

---

**The four cuts I made, and why I'm confident about each:**

The high school tutoring program went. It's on your resume, it's the oldest item in the essay, and in a paragraph arguing "I improve organizations," a high school tutoring program is the weakest of the three examples — the unused dashboard is doing far more work.

"I automated the work without being able to analyze it" went, because the very next sentence said the same thing better. Two sentences, one idea, and the vivid one survived.

Same logic in the fail-closed paragraph: "when anything is wrong, the campaign does not launch and explains why" and "the worst outcome is that nothing happens" are the same principle stated twice. I kept the memorable one, which also defines "fail closed" for a reader who doesn't know the term.

The rest was small — Fort Lauderdale folded into the company description, "a single class" to "a class," and similar.

**One flag on the certificate line.** I wrote "plan to pursue" rather than "am pursuing," because you said you *wanted* to, which reads like you haven't applied. If you have applied, change it to "am pursuing" — it's stronger. Don't upgrade the verb if you haven't.

Want the Word doc and the email to msisom@warrington.ufl.edu?

You're right, and I can name exactly what's wrong. Three real problems:

**"That instinct is not new"** points backward at the wrong thing. The paragraph before it is about restraint — knowing when *not* to automate. But the examples that follow are about building things. So the pronoun promises one idea and delivers another.

**The Homecoming sentence doesn't belong there.** Managing a website isn't finding an inefficiency. It's sitting in a paragraph about spotting broken things and doing nothing to support the claim.

**The dashboard is a counterexample dressed as an example.** I introduce it as proof of an instinct, then describe it as a thing that didn't work. That's the sentence that makes the whole passage feel like it's arguing against itself.

Here's the fix — two paragraphs rewritten, nothing else touched:

> It did not work at first. Early drafts invented statistics that sounded plausible and were false. The fix was not a better prompt but a system designed to fail closed. The worst outcome is that nothing happens, never that the wrong thing sends. I also left lead selection manual on purpose, because that is where human judgment earns its keep.
>
> Not everything I built worked. I also made an operations dashboard that nobody used. It ran exactly as designed and solved a problem the company did not have, which taught me that building something well is not the same as building the right thing. That is the part of this work I keep coming back to: finding what is actually broken, and fixing it in a way that leaves an organization better organized than I found it. My supervisor asked me to return this fall as Director of Interns.

Now the second paragraph is honestly framed as a failure, the failure produces a lesson, the lesson states your through-line, and the Director of Interns line has a reason to be in the sentence it's in.

**I cut Homecoming, and I want to flag that rather than slip it past you.** I couldn't find a place where it earned its keep without making the paragraph muddy again — which was your original complaint. It's on your resume. If you want it back in, the honest version is a separate short sentence somewhere, but I'd advise against it.

One other small thing I noticed while re-reading: in the opening, "I had always been good with computers, and it seemed like the closest fit" — "it" technically points at "computers," not "computer science." I'd change it to "and computer science seemed like the obvious fit."

I want to make the case for keeping something there, because that sentence is doing more work than it looks like.

The essay's whole argument is that you need this degree. If you can already do everything, the program has no job. But building the system is only half the job. The other half is knowing why one campaign outperformed another and proving it with data. That is the half I want next.

The gap is still named — a reader understands exactly what the degree gives you — but it's framed as appetite rather than admission. "That is the half I want next" is a person reaching for something, not confessing a limit.

The next paragraph then opens "That is why I want the MS-ISOM" instead of "That gap is why."

---

Benjamin Jones
Statement of Purpose
MS in Information Systems and Operations Management, Data Science Concentration (Combination Degree)

I applied to the University of Florida as a computer science major. I had always been good with computers, and it seemed like the obvious fit. Before I started, I spoke with a UF senior who had just accepted a project management job at American Express. That was the job I wanted, and he was not a computer science major. He studied information systems, a degree I had never heard of.

I looked it up that night and found a field described as the bridge between business needs and technology. I had spent months assuming that working in technology meant becoming a great programmer. What I actually wanted was to decide what got built and why. I changed my major at Preview, before taking a class at UF.

This past summer I worked as a Marketing and Data Operations Apprentice at Global Source Data Solutions, a data-as-a-service company managing datasets across 150 countries. I did not expect an incoming sophomore to be useful there. Then I noticed that launching one email campaign took most of a day, with a busy sales team as the bottleneck on work that was mostly mechanical. Nobody asked me to fix that. I built what the team now calls the Campaign Factory: anyone fills out a two-minute form, an automated pipeline drafts the sequence in the company's voice and checks it against our compliance rules, and it posts to Slack as a proposal that cannot send without a director's approval. A campaign now takes minutes instead of a day.

It did not work at first. Early drafts invented statistics that sounded plausible and were false. The fix was not a better prompt. It was designing the system so that anything wrong stops a campaign instead of sending it. The worst outcome became nothing happening, never the wrong thing going out. I also left lead selection manual on purpose, where human judgment earns its keep.

Not everything I built worked. I also made an operations dashboard that nobody used. It ran exactly as designed and solved a problem the company did not have, which taught me that building something well is not the same as building the right thing. That is what I keep coming back to: finding what is actually broken and fixing it so an organization runs better than I found it. The CEO asked me to return this fall as Director of Interns.

The summer also showed me where I want to go deeper. I learn by jumping in; I had no experience with these tools in May, and by August I could teach them. But building the system is only half the job. The other half is knowing why one campaign outperformed another and proving it with data. That is the half I want next.

That is why I want the MS-ISOM with a Data Science concentration. I do not want to leave UF with only a general understanding of how businesses use technology. Courses like Data Mining for Business Intelligence and Business Data Presentation and Visualization, plus the business analytics practicum, would give me the foundation behind instincts I have been running on. I am also pursuing UF's certificate in AI Fundamentals and Applications, in part for its coursework in data ethics, which matters in an industry built on other people's information. The combination degree lets me start now rather than graduating, working several years, and coming back later.

Long term, I want to run operations at a data company, which is what my supervisor does. Warrington is where I learned that job existed, and where I want to become qualified for it.

---

To absorb the three extra words, I made small trims elsewhere: "because that is where human judgment" became "where human judgment," and "found it described as the field that bridges the gap between" became "found a field described as the bridge between." Nothing else moved.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 20:41

Honest answer: roughly **75%** of the words as they now stand were typed by me first.

Here's the breakdown, because the number alone is misleading in both directions.

**Mine, word for word:** "Nobody had asked me to fix it." "Not everything I built worked." "Building something well isn't the same as building the right thing." "Building the system is only half the job." The entire Campaign Factory description. The fail-closed sentence. The data ethics sentence. The closing three sentences, though you corrected the last one for accuracy. Most of the connective tissue in paragraphs one and two.

**Yours, word for word or nearly:** "I learn by jumping in" — your exact phrase from my question 18. "I do not want to leave UF with only a general understanding of how businesses use technology" — almost verbatim from what you wrote me for question 17, as is the combination degree sentence after it. "Find what is actually broken and build something that makes an organization run better" is a light rewrite of your own through-line answer. And your edits across the last several turns — "great coder," "should be quick," the semicolon, "Vice President of Operations' approval," "before even taking a class" — are all yours.

**Yours entirely, in a way the percentage doesn't capture:** every fact. The Meta conversation, the Preview switch, noticing the bottleneck, the decision to build it, the n8n errors, the unused dashboard and *why* it went unused, the CEO asking you back. I didn't invent a single event. When you asked me to make something up for question 17, I declined, and you wrote it yourself.

Also worth noting: the last six turns have been you rejecting my sentences. The confusing paragraph, the "I cannot tell you," the colon, the throat-clearing opener, the false closing line. Every one of those was a real problem and you caught it. That's editorial judgment, and it's why the essay reads like you.

**What I'd do with that number.** 75% is high enough that you should be comfortable answering the question if it's ever asked, and it's worth deciding now what your answer is. Two practical things: if they interview you, you'll need to talk about the Campaign Factory in your own words with no draft in front of you — so make sure you can explain the fail-closed design out loud. And if you'd feel uneasy telling Daniel or the program how this was written, that's worth sitting with before you send it, not after.

The version that would be most defensibly yours is one where you retype it from scratch without looking at this thread. You know the material well enough now. It'd take twenty minutes and it would probably be better.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 20:44

Three candidates, and I'm confident about two.

**Cut this one.** "I was tasked with building internal tools and helping with email campaigns." It's a job-description sentence in an essay that otherwise shows rather than tells, and the next four sentences demonstrate exactly this. It also sits awkwardly between "I did not expect to be useful there" and "I noticed the bottleneck" — those two connect directly, and this interrupts them. Saves 12 words.

**Cut this one too.** "That is the work I want to keep doing." It's a hinge sentence announcing the point, and then the very next sentence makes the point. Two sentences, one job. Drop it and "I want to find what is actually broken and build something that makes an organization run better" lands right after the dashboard lesson, which is where it belongs. Saves 9.

**This one is a judgment call, and I'd keep it.** "I had always been good with computers, and it seemed like the obvious fit." You could argue it's throat-clearing. But it explains why you picked CS in the first place, and without it the opening is just "I applied as CS, then I switched," which makes the switch feel arbitrary. I'd leave it.

One more thing I'd flag that isn't a cut: **"Long-term"** starts a new idea about your career but sits in the middle of a paragraph about coursework. Give it its own paragraph. It's your conclusion and right now it's buried.

Those two cuts put you at 574, which gives you 26 words of room if you decide something needs to come back.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 21:00

Good instinct to check. Let me verify each one against the source.

> *Claude used web_fetch: https://warrington.ufl.edu/graduate/masters-information-systems-operations-management-data-science/*

All three verified. Everything is on the official Data Science concentration page.

**Data Mining for Business Intelligence** — listed under Core courses. Described as understanding how data analytics are applied to interact effectively with CTOs, data miners and business analysts.

**Business Data Presentation and Visualization** — also Core. Described as understanding key data visualization principles and applying them to real-world business problems.

**Business analytics practicum** — listed under "Integrated experience and career preparation." It's an optional summer semester assignment that places you with one of Warrington's business partners, where you draw on modeling, programming and systems design skills to address and present a solution to a challenge.

Source: https://warrington.ufl.edu/graduate/masters-information-systems-operations-management-data-science/ — scroll to the "Courses" section for the first two and "Integrated experience and career preparation" for the practicum.

Two things worth knowing beyond the strict accuracy check:

The page describes the concentration as containing a five-course data science sequence covering database systems, business intelligence, data mining and data visualization — so both courses you named sit inside that named sequence. That's a good sign you picked representative ones rather than obscure electives.

The practicum is **optional**, and your sentence implies these are things you'd take. "Along with the business analytics practicum" is fine as written since you're describing what the program offers, not promising enrollment. But if an interviewer asks, know that it's an elective summer placement, not a required capstone.

One flag while I have the page open: you're applying to the **combination** track, and the requirements you're being judged against are the ones on the combination degrees page — 3.2 cumulative UF GPA, 60 credit hours, Universal Tracking complete — not the 3.0 upper-division GPA listed on this page, which applies to traditional applicants holding a bachelor's. Your September 1 deadline comes from the combination page too.

Statement of Purpose

I applied to the University of Florida to major in computer science. I had always been good with computers and thought that was the best path. Before I started, I spoke with a UF senior who had just accepted a project management job at Meta. That was the job I wanted, and she wasn't a computer science major. She was studying information systems, which was a degree I had never heard of. I looked it up that night and found it described as the field that bridges the gap between business and technology. I spent months assuming that working in technology meant becoming a great coder, but what I actually wanted was to decide what got built. I changed my major at Preview before even taking a class at UF.
This past summer, I worked as a Marketing and Data Operations Intern at Global Source Data Solutions, a data-as-a-service company managing datasets across 150 countries. I did not expect an incoming sophomore to be useful there. They told me to build internal tools and help with email campaigns. I noticed that launching one email campaign took me most of my day, with a busy sales team as the bottleneck on work that should be quick. Nobody had asked me to fix it, but I built them a Campaign Factory. The team fills out a quick form and uploads a list of prospective leads. Our automated campaign pipeline creates a campaign in the company's brand tone and compliant with campaign rules, then posts it to Slack for review by the Vice President of Operations. Now they take minutes instead of a day. It didn’t work at first. The factory would come up with statistics that sounded great but were entirely false. To fix this, we created a campaign that wouldn't let us say anything false.
Not everything I built worked. I also made an operations dashboard that nobody used. While it fulfilled a need in the company, it wasn't a problem they had. Building something well is not the same as building the right thing. Instead, I want to focus on what is broken in existing systems and build something that helps a business run better. At the end of the summer, the CEO asked me to come back this fall as Director of Interns. 
I learn by jumping in. I had no experience with these tools in May, and by August I could teach them. Building the system was half the job; the other half is understanding why one campaign performed better than another. That is why I want to learn how to analyze the data behind these campaigns next.

That is why the MS-ISOM with a Data Science concentration is the degree that will best help me reach my goals at UF. Courses like Data Mining for Business Intelligence and Business Data Presentation and Visualization will allow me to learn the technical aspects of business analytics. I am also pursuing UF’s certificate in AI Fundamentals and Applications, which will help me learn more about data ethics, which is crucial in my industry. The combined degree lets me start that work now rather than waiting until after I graduate and begin working in the field for several years.  I want to run operations at a data company, which is what my supervisor does. I learned that job existed by working for someone who does it. The MS-ISOM is how I get ready to do it myself.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 21:05

> *Claude used bash_tool: mkdir -p /home/claude/sop && cat > /home/claude/sop/v5.txt << 'EOF' I applied to the University of Florida to major in computer science. I had always been good with computers and thought that was the ...*

576 words, so length is fine. But you asked me to be an expert, so here's the honest read: **this version is weaker than your last one**, and three of the changes cost you real points.

**Verdict on admission:** I'd still expect you to get in, but on the strength of your record rather than this essay. Your GPA, the internship, the CEO asking you back — that carries it. This draft doesn't help as much as the previous one did, and one sentence in it actively hurts.

---

## The three that matter

**1. "took me most of my day" — this is the costly one.** Your previous version said launching one campaign "took most of a day," which described a company bottleneck you spotted and eliminated. Now it says it took *you* most of *your* day. You've converted your best insight into an admission that you were slow at a task. The whole point of that paragraph is that you saw an organizational problem nobody had assigned you. Change it back to "took most of a day."

**2. "we created a campaign that wouldn't let us say anything false."** This sentence doesn't parse, and it's the last line of your strongest paragraph. A campaign can't prevent falsehood — a *system* can. And "we" gives away credit you earned. You built it. Something like: "So I rebuilt it so that anything unverified stops the campaign before it sends."

**3. "While it fulfilled a need in the company, it wasn't a problem they had."** This contradicts itself — if it filled a need, it was a problem they had. You mean it worked perfectly and solved something nobody needed solved. As written, a careful reader stops and re-reads, which is the last thing you want two-thirds of the way through.

---

## The smaller ones, in order

"They told me to build internal tools" is passive, and it sits four sentences before "Nobody had asked me to fix it." One of those two undercuts the other.

"compliant with campaign rules" was previously "compliance rules," which meant legal compliance — CAN-SPAM, opt-outs, the voter-file rules. "Campaign rules" sounds like internal style preferences. You've made a serious safeguard sound trivial.

You dropped "cannot be sent without approval" in favor of "posts it to Slack for review." The approval gate *is* the design decision. Review is passive; a hard gate is a choice you made.

"Instead, I want to focus on..." — instead of what? The previous sentence is a lesson, not an alternative, so "instead" has nothing to attach to.

"That is why" ends one paragraph and opens the next. Change one.

"the degree that will best help me reach my goals at UF" — your goals aren't at UF, they're after it. Cut "at UF."

"which is crucial in my industry" — you've had one internship. "My industry" overclaims, and it's vaguer than what you had before.

"decide what got built" lost "and why." The "why" was the part that distinguished you from a project manager.

---

## What you improved

"I built them a Campaign Factory" is better than "what they now call the Campaign Factory" — more direct, less hedged. Breaking the pipeline into separate sentences reads more cleanly than the semicolon version. And cutting the practicum was right if you don't want it; you don't owe them enthusiasm you don't have.

---

My honest recommendation: go back to your previous draft and port over only the two improvements above. That version was tighter, more accurate, and made you look sharper. This one reads like it was edited for variety rather than for meaning — which is a normal thing to happen on the fifth pass, and a good reason to stop passing.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 21:06

The contradiction is in "fulfilled a need." Swap that for what actually happened — it worked, but the need wasn't there:

```
It worked exactly the way I designed it, but the company didn't actually have that problem.
```

Or if you want the "well built" idea inside the sentence:

```
It was built well; the company just didn't need it.
```

The first is closer to your voice and sets up your next line better, since "building something well is not the same as building the right thing" then reads as the lesson drawn from it rather than a restatement.

Full paragraph with that swap:

> Not everything I built worked. I also made an operations dashboard that nobody used. It worked exactly the way I designed it, but the company didn't actually have that problem. Building something well is not the same as building the right thing. Instead, I want to focus on what is broken in existing systems and build something that helps a business run better. At the end of the summer, the CEO asked me to come back this fall as Director of Interns.

While you're in there, "Instead" still has nothing to point back to. Deleting the word alone fixes it — "I want to focus on what is broken in existing systems and build something that helps a business run better" works as a standalone sentence.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 21:09

Much better. The three big ones are fixed, and the dashboard paragraph now reads cleanly.

A few things left, in order of how much they cost you.

**"took me most of a day"** — you swapped "my day" for "a day" but left the "me." It still reads as your personal speed rather than a company bottleneck.

```
took most of a day
```

**"in the company's brand tone and compliant with compliance rules"** — "compliant with compliance" is a stutter, and the sentence has "campaign pipeline creates a campaign."

```
Our automated pipeline drafts the emails in the company's brand tone, checks them against our compliance rules, then posts them to Slack for the Vice President of Operations to review.
```

**"That is why" twice in a row**, ending one paragraph and opening the next. Change the second:

```
The MS-ISOM with a Data Science concentration is the degree that will best help me reach my goals.
```

That also drops the misplaced "at UF" — your goals come after UF, not at it.

**"which will help me learn more about data ethics, which is crucial in my industry"** — two "which" clauses stacked, and "my industry" overclaims on one internship.

```
which includes coursework in data ethics, something that matters in an industry built on other people's information.
```

**"They told me to build internal tools"** — still passive, and it sits four sentences before "Nobody had asked me to fix it," which is your best line. I'd just cut the sentence. The paragraph loses nothing.

---

Two smaller notes I'd leave to you. "compliance rules" is fine after the rewrite above, but if a reader asks what that means, know your answer — footers, opt-out language, the voter-file rules. And "anything false stops the campaign" is good, though "anything unverified" is more accurate to how it actually works, since the system can't know what's false, only what it can't confirm.

Make those and you're at roughly 570. The essay is in good shape — this is polish, not repair.

This is good. It's ready to send after two small fixes.

**"campaign pipeline creates a campaign"** — still the stutter. Also "creates a campaign in the company's brand tone and follows compliance rules" has a grammar mismatch: "creates a campaign... and follows" makes the pipeline the subject of both verbs, which works, but "in the company's brand tone and follows compliance rules" reads as a mismatched pair.

```
Our automated pipeline drafts the emails in the company's brand tone, checks them against our compliance rules, then posts them to Slack for the Vice President of Operations to review.
```

**"which will help me learn more about data ethics and is crucial in my industry"** — the "is crucial" now attaches to the certificate rather than to data ethics, which isn't what you mean. And "my industry" still overclaims on one summer.

```
which includes coursework in data ethics, something that matters in an industry built on other people's information.
```

That's it. Everything else I'd leave alone.

Two things I'd still flag but won't push on, since they're yours to decide:

"They told me to build internal tools" sits four sentences before "Nobody had asked me to fix it." A reader may notice the tension. Cutting it costs nothing, but it's defensible if you want the scope of the role established up front.

"Anything false stops the campaign" — the system actually catches unverified claims, not false ones, since it can't know truth. It's close enough for an essay, but know the real answer if it comes up in conversation.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 21:14

Fair question — let me check the actual requirement rather than trust my earlier summary.

> *Claude used web_fetch: https://catalog.ufl.edu/UGRD/academic-programs/AI/aicertificate*

Verified, and I need to correct one thing I told you earlier.

**Yes, ethics is a required component.** The certificate is three courses, 9 credits, and one of them is a required ethics course. From the catalog:

- **EEL 3872** Artificial Intelligence Fundamentals (3 cr)
- **PHI 3681** Ethics, Data, and Technology (3 cr) — required, not optional
- One college-specific course (3 cr). For Business students that's **QMB 3302** Foundations of Business Analytics and Artificial Intelligence

Source: https://catalog.ufl.edu/UGRD/academic-programs/AI/aicertificate/

So "which includes coursework in data ethics" is accurate. The course is literally called Ethics, Data, and Technology.

**My correction:** earlier I gave you the course number as PHI 3861. It's **PHI 3681**. That came from a 2022 UF flyer that had it transposed. The catalog is authoritative. It doesn't affect your essay since you don't cite the number, but if it comes up in conversation or you go to register, use 3681.

**Two things worth knowing while I'm here.** Admission requires junior standing or higher plus MAC 1147 or equivalent — so you can't start this immediately. And you must apply for the certificate before taking your second course, then apply to graduate with it. If QMB 3302 is already on your Business track and you take it without applying first, you may have sequencing problems.

That second point matters for your essay. "I am also pursuing" is a present-tense claim, and if you can't enroll until junior standing, it may be more accurate to say "I plan to pursue." Only you know where you stand on credits — you said you've hit 60, which may or may not put you at junior standing this term. Worth a two-minute check before you send.

### Claude · 2026-08-22 21:20

This is a question on the combination degree application form, and it's one I can't answer for you — I don't know what programming coursework you've actually taken.

What I know from our conversations: AP Computer Science in high school, and the tooling from GSDSI (n8n, Vercel, Slack and Instantly APIs, Anthropic API, GitHub, Supabase, Lovable). Your resume lists GitHub and AI tools but no programming languages, and I don't know your UF course history.

A word of caution on framing. This question exists because the program recommends applicants take a programming course, and they're gauging readiness. Listing AI-assisted tool building as "programming experience" without saying so would be a mischaracterization — and it's exactly the kind of thing that becomes awkward if it comes up. But you also shouldn't undersell: building and debugging an n8n pipeline with API integrations is real technical work, it's just honestly described as automation and integration rather than software development.

Here's the shape I'd use, with your actual details filled in:

> **Coursework:** AP Computer Science [A or Principles — which one?], [high school year]. [Any UF courses — ISM 3232, COP 2271, QMB 3302?]
>
> **Applied experience:** Built an automated marketing pipeline at Global Source Data Solutions using n8n, integrating the Instantly, Slack, and Anthropic APIs, with a validation layer that halts execution on unverified output. Debugged workflow failures and iterated on the system across the summer. Version control via GitHub. Front-end work on the UF Homecoming and Gator Growl websites using HTML and CSS. Much of the build work was AI-assisted rather than written from scratch.
>
> **Self-directed:** [any Udemy, Codecademy, freeCodeCamp, Khan Academy — did you do any?]

That last line in the applied section is the one I'd keep. Saying it yourself reads as self-aware; having them infer it reads as padding.

**Two things I need from you to finish this:** which UF programming or IS courses you've taken or are registered for this fall, and whether you've done any self-directed courses. Also, was your AP Computer Science course A or Principles, and did you take the exam?

Researched it, and there's something you need to know before you copy that LinkedIn.

**Don't use the $1 million+ figure.** UF's Activity and Service Fee budget allocated ACCENT $497,750 for FY 2022-23 — roughly half what that profile claims. The Alligator has reported ACCENT's annual speaking-fee spending ranged from $62,500 to $463,000 across a fifteen-year period. ACCENT's budget has also been the subject of public records requests and student journalism scrutiny, which means it's a number people actually look up. Putting an inflated figure on a resume submitted to UF, about a UF agency, is a bad risk for a small gain.

**Assistant Comptroller** *(August 2026 – Present)*

### Claude · 2026-08-22 21:41

Two ways, and they trade off differently.

**Option A — collapse into one entry.** Cleanest for VMock's parser and probably what it wants:

**ACCENT Speakers Bureau, University of Florida Student Government** — Gainesville, FL
**Assistant Comptroller** *(May 2026 – Present)*
- Serve on the executive board of the nation's largest student-run speakers bureau, founded in 1967 and funded through UF's Activity and Service Fee budget.
- Process expense reports and reimbursements for campus speaking events, ensuring compliance with university financial policy and SG fiscal code.
- Promoted from Social Media Director after managing ACCENT's social presence and event promotion across platforms for one term.

The promotion lives in a bullet instead of a date range. Parsers read bullets fine; they choke on stacked titles under one org.

**Option B — two separate entries**, each with its own org line, dates, and bullets. More space, but nothing breaks and the progression is visible at a glance.

---

**My honest take: don't optimize this for VMock.** VMock is an automated scorer trained on generic recruiting resumes. It doesn't know that UF's own instructions for this application say the resume isn't limited to one page and explicitly ask you to elaborate on leadership activities and time commitments. A human in the MS-ISOM office reads yours, and that human is not running a parser.

You've now made several changes to satisfy a tool — cutting to fit one page, restructuring a real promotion — for an application whose stated instructions point the opposite direction. VMock is useful for catching weak verbs and missing numbers. It's not the audience.

I'd take Option A because it's genuinely tidier, not because VMock prefers it. And I'd stop letting the score drive decisions from here.

One formatting error and a few things to verify.

**Formatting — fix this before sending.** Paragraph four runs straight into paragraph five with no break: `...analyze the data behind these campaigns next.The MS-ISOM with a Data Science concentration...`. Missing space and missing paragraph break. Most visible error in the document.

**Verify these yourself — I can't:**

**Meta.** You told me American Express early on, then Meta. Only one is true.

**"a data-as-a-service company managing datasets across 150 countries."** Your resume says 150+ countries and your internship reflection said over 150. Fine as written, just confirm.

**"the CEO asked me to come back this fall as Director of Interns."** You said the CEO offered it. Confirm it was the CEO and not Daniel, since the essay names the role specifically.

**"I am also pursuing UF's certificate in AI Fundamentals and Applications."** Requires junior standing plus MAC 1147. If you can't enroll yet, "plan to pursue" is the accurate verb.

**Consistent with your resume — good.** Intern matches. Data Mining for Business Intelligence and Business Data Presentation and Visualization are both real core courses on the Data Science page. The certificate does include a required ethics course, PHI 3681 Ethics, Data, and Technology.

**One imprecision worth knowing about even if you leave it:** "anything false stops the campaign" — the system catches unverified claims, not false ones, since it can't know truth. Fine for an essay, but have the real answer ready if someone asks.

**Two small wording things, optional:**

"My bosses told me to build internal tools" — plural bosses reads oddly, and it's four sentences before "Nobody had asked me to fix it." Cutting the sentence costs nothing.

"something which is crucial" → "something that is crucial."

Fix the paragraph break, confirm Meta, and it's ready.

### Claude · 2026-08-25 15:39

Before craft notes, I have to raise something, because it affects whether this letter helps or hurts you.

This letter follows my reference sheet almost exactly — not just in content, but in sequence and phrasing. My brief listed five areas in order: readiness for graduate work now, how he handled being wrong, communication growth (June weakness, July strength), what he was like to manage day to day, comparison to peers. The letter hits all five in that order.

And some of the language is mine or yours, not Daniel's:

- My brief: *"whether a second-year student is ready for graduate work now."* The letter: *"whether a second-year student is ready for graduate work now."*
- My brief suggested *"strongest intern we've had"* or *"a comparison to early-career full-time staff"* — both appear, nearly verbatim.
- *"Fail closed"* is the phrasing I introduced in your essay drafting.
- Your essay: *"I cannot yet tell you, with rigor, why one campaign outperformed another."* The letter: *"He cannot yet tell you rigorously why one campaign outperformed another."*

A recommendation letter is an attestation by the recommender. If Daniel wrote this in his own voice after reading the brief, the overlap is still a problem — an admissions reader who sees the letter echo the personal statement discounts both. But it's fixable.

**If Daniel didn't write it, stop.** Submitting a letter under someone's name that they didn't author is falsification of an application document. UF can rescind admission for it, at any point, including after you've enrolled. And Daniel is your current employer who's expecting you back in three weeks as Director of Interns — this is his signature and his professional credibility, not just yours.

I'm not assuming which situation this is. But the letter arrived the same day I wrote the brief, dated today, tracking it point by point, and I'd be doing you a disservice to review the prose and say nothing.

**So: did Daniel write this?**

If yes, here's what I'd tell him to change:

Cut the phrases that overlap with the application — "fail closed," the rigor line, "second-year student ready for graduate work." Same points in his own words. Add one thing only he saw: a specific meeting, a decision you made in front of him, something not in your essay. The June/July communication detail comes from your self-assessment paperwork; if he uses it, he should describe what he actually observed instead of citing the rating. And "I recommend him without reservation" followed later by "I will name the gap" is a small contradiction worth smoothing.

If no — tell me, and we'll figure out the right way to ask him for a real one. There's still time before September 1.
