### Claude · 2026-09-24 14:54

That's fine, and it's more common than you'd think. The move is to quantify the **build**, not the results. You have real countable facts — three founders, a 20-minute diagnostic, five target industries, four service lines you own — and none of them require a client to be true.

**Volvera AI** — Co-Founder · July 2026 – Present · Miami, FL

```
Co-founded a three-person AI consultancy that opens every engagement with a free 20-minute funnel audit, naming and sizing a company's single largest revenue leak so the build that follows is scoped to a diagnosed problem rather than sold as a package.

Own the marketing infrastructure function across five target industries, building the target lists, lifecycle email, review capture and newsletter programs that make up four of the firm's service lines, plus the CRM and website work underneath them.

Built an outbound email system that draws target lists from a client's own closed-won customer profile rather than a bought database, then writes per-segment sequences scored on reply rate instead of open rate, the only signal that maps to booked revenue.

Built the review-capture flow that fires a tap-to-open request the moment a job closes while the customer still feels it, and rebuild client CRMs from scratch so the pipeline reconciles with what reps actually did rather than what they remembered to log.
```

All four run 249–254 characters, so both lines fill evenly.

**Why this is actually the safer version.** A recruiter reading a founder entry on a sophomore's resume is asking one question: is this real, or is it a LinkedIn headline? Describing systems you built in specific technical language answers that. Implied traction you don't have does the opposite the moment someone asks a follow-up.

**And they will ask.** Have this ready: *"We launched in July and we're pre-revenue — we're building the service catalog and running funnel reads now. I own the marketing infrastructure side."* That's a completely normal answer for a two-month-old company. What isn't normal is a resume that suggests clients and a founder who can't name one.

**When the first number lands,** it goes in bullet one. First funnel read delivered, first client signed, first campaign shipped — whichever comes first.

**Volvera AI** — Co-Founder · July 2026 – Present · Miami, FL

```
Co-founded a three-person AI consultancy that audits a company's sales funnel for free before quoting anything, finds the single place they are losing the most revenue, and then builds only the system that fixes it rather than a package of services.

Build each client's cold email program end to end, from a prospect list modeled on the customers they have already closed, to separate sequences written for each group, to the reply tracking that shows which version is actually booking meetings.
```

244 to 254 characters each, so both lines fill.

Nothing explains why it matters, because a recruiter reading "shows what reps actually did instead of what they remembered to log" already knows why that matters.

Read it. Here's the verdict and what to change.

## The honest read

The GSDSI block is the strongest thing on the page. Real tools, real numbers, real scale. A recruiter skimming for six seconds gets "automation, data, 301M records, 3,000 prospects" and stops. That block is done.

The Volvera block is the weak half. Four bullets, zero numbers, and bullet one describes the *company's business model* rather than anything you did. "Audits a company's sales funnel for free before quoting anything" is a sales pitch, not an accomplishment, and "for free" is the last thing you want a recruiter's eye landing on. The writing is also noticeably more conversational than everything above it ("while they still feel good about it," "instead of just look good"), which makes the page read like two different people wrote it.

Tense is scrambled inside that one block: **Co-founded / Build / Built...and run / Rebuild.** Four bullets, three tenses.

## Why the lines look uneven (this is a formatting bug, not a writing problem)

I measured the actual PDF geometry. Your document has **two different right indents**:

| Block | Right edge | Column width |
|---|---|---|
| GSDSI + ACCENT | 547 pt | 6.86" |
| Volvera, Student Gov, Homecoming, Florida Tech | 578 pt | 7.28" |

That 0.43" gap is the whole problem. I wrote those bullets to fill a 6.86" column, and half of them are sitting in a 7.28" column, so their second lines land at 70-83% fill while GSDSI lands at 96-99%.

**Fix in Google Docs:** select every bullet in the document → Format → Align & indent → Indentation options → set **Right indent to 0.43"**. The GSDSI and ACCENT bullets already have it; this just brings the rest in line.

Once that's set, with the swaps below every bullet on the page lands at 91-99% fill on its second line.

## Copy-paste replacements

**Volvera AI (all four, present tense, you as the subject):**

```
Co-founded a three-person AI consultancy and own the marketing infrastructure side of it, building the cold email, review request, newsletter, CRM and landing page systems that every client engagement is scoped, priced and delivered on.

Build each client's cold email program end to end, from a prospect list modeled on the customers they have already closed, to separate sequences written for each group, to the reply tracking that shows which version is actually booking meetings.

Built a review system that texts customers a one-tap review link the minute their job is marked complete, while the work is still fresh, and run the weekly newsletter that keeps every lead who quoted but never booked in front of the client.

Rebuild client CRMs so the pipeline reflects what reps actually did instead of what they remembered to log, and rebuild the landing pages their paid ads point at so the traffic they are already paying for lands somewhere built to convert.
```

**Three leadership bullets that come up short in the fixed column:**

```
Represent the Warrington College of Business and its students in the UF Student Senate, contributing to legislation that governs a $24M student activity budget and the funding of more than 1,000 registered student organizations on campus.

Review, debate and vote on legislation alongside 99 fellow senators under formal parliamentary procedure, allocating activity fee funding and approving the student initiatives that shape campus life for roughly 60,000 students each year.

Manage and update the Homecoming and Gator Growl websites using HTML, CSS and UX best practices, collaborating with 10+ cross-functional teams to ship content, features and promotional updates on deadline at every stage of the season.
```

## Small fixes that cost you credibility

**Education block.** Three things on the most-read four lines of the page:
- `Minor: AI Certificate, Professional Selling Certificate` → **`Certificates: AI Fundamentals and Applications, Professional Selling`**. Those are certificates, not minors, and anyone who knows UF will notice.
- `May 2029` → **`Expected May 2029`**. Recruiters gate on this date; don't make them guess.
- `Gainesville, Florida` → **`Gainesville, FL`**. Everything else on the page uses the abbreviation.

**SQL and Excel appear in a bullet but nowhere in Technical Skills.** That is a real ATS miss. Excel is the single most-filtered keyword for business analyst roles and it is not on your page. Add both.

**"Film Digitization"** is orphaned now that the job it came from is gone, and it reads strangely next to API Integration. Cut it or move it to Interests.

**Revised skills lines:**

```
Technical Skills: SQL, Excel, AI Tools (Claude, Gemini, Lovable), n8n Automation, API Integration, GitHub, HTML/CSS
Marketing Skills: Email Campaigns, Social Media, CRMs, Technical SEO, Monda Data Marketplace, Lead Generation
```

## Two things worth adding

**Relevant Coursework.** You have about two lines of vertical space left before the bottom margin. As a second-year, coursework is one of the only signals you have that you can do the analytical work, and the MS-ISOM data science track makes it doubly relevant. One line under Education:

```
Relevant Coursework: [your actual courses here]
```

Fill it with what you have actually taken or are taking now. Don't guess.

**Trim Interests to four or five items.** Eight is a lot of real estate for the least-read line on the page, and cutting three buys you the coursework line.

## One judgment call

Both jobs are current, so there is no strict reverse-chronological order between them. Keep GSDSI first. It is the block with tools, numbers and verifiable scale, and it is the one that maps to the analyst and data roles you are targeting. Volvera earns its place second as the founder story.

Also worth a quick check: your Statement of Purpose and this resume should use the same GSDSI title. Make sure both say **Marketing & Data Operations Intern**.

One note without relitigating anything: after the indent fix, the last Florida Tech Pathways bullet will be the shortest line on the page at about 78% fill. Say the word whenever you want to rebuild that one and I'll size it to match.

### Claude · 2026-09-24 18:58

> *Claude used WebSearch: Raymond James summer associate intern hourly pay St. Petersburg*

> *Claude used WebSearch: Raymond James internship salary per hour Glassdoor 2026*

> *Claude used WebFetch: https://www.indeed.com/cmp/Raymond-James/salaries/Intern/Florida*

> *Claude used WebFetch: https://www.glassdoor.com/Hourly-Pay/Raymond-James-Financial-Intern-Florida-Hourly-Pay-EJI_IE559.0,31_KO24,30_IL.31,38_IS3318.htm*

Short version: this field is a screen, not a negotiation. Intern pay at a firm like Raymond James is a fixed band set before the req was posted, so there is no number you can write that gets you more money. There are only numbers that get you flagged. Answer it to look market-aware and easy to work with, then move on.

## What to write

**If it's a free-text box (most likely):**

```
Open to the standard rate for the Summer Associate program. For comparable
summer roles in the St. Petersburg market I'd expect roughly $20-$25 per hour,
and I'm flexible within whatever band the program uses.
```

**If it's a short field with a character limit:**

```
$20-$25/hour, flexible and open to the program's standard rate.
```

**If it forces a single number with no unit:** check the field's placeholder or helper text first. If it says hourly, put `22`. If it's clearly an annual salary field, put `45,000` (the annualized equivalent). If there is genuinely no unit anywhere, write the number with the unit if the field accepts any text at all, because a bare `22` in an annual field reads as a typo and a bare `45000` in an hourly field reads as delusional.

## Why $20-$25

Indeed's data for Raymond James interns in Florida shows an average of **$23.08/hour**, with postings spanning $15 to $34.85. The top of that range is engineering and quant work; marketing, communications and analyst roles sit in the middle. Over a 9-week, 40-hour program that's roughly **$7,200 to $9,000 for the summer**.

Note that the "salary + bonus" phrasing is boilerplate lifted from their full-time requisition template. This is an hourly, temporary role and there is no bonus component, which is why answering in hourly terms is the sharper move.

## Four ways to get this wrong

- **Blank, "N/A," or "0."** Reads as careless on a form that takes ten seconds.
- **"Negotiable" with no number.** Some recruiters read a bare "negotiable" as evasive. Flexible *plus* a number is the combination you want.
- **Anchoring high to leave room.** There is no room. You'd just be the one candidate whose expectations don't match the band.
- **Going too low.** Writing $15 or "whatever you offer" doesn't buy you goodwill and does signal you haven't looked at the market. It cannot help you get the job.

## One thing on this posting that will get you auto-rejected if you miss it

Buried in Educational/Previous Experience:

> Must submit a cover letter expressing your interest in Raymond James Marketing.

That is a hard requirement, and it's the single most common reason applications to this req get screened out. Make sure the cover letter is attached and that it names Raymond James Marketing specifically, not just "your firm."

Also, confirming the eligibility line for this one since it was the open question before: sophomore or junior standing, graduation date **May 2028 or later**. You're a second-year graduating May 2029. Clean fit, no exception needed.

Sources: [Raymond James intern hourly pay in Florida (Indeed)](https://www.indeed.com/cmp/Raymond-James/salaries/Intern/Florida)

### Claude · 2026-09-24 18:59

It's not going to get you rejected, but it's the weakest usable answer. Two reasons.

First, "competitive" is a word with no content. It's also the exact word companies use in job postings *to avoid* disclosing pay, so it reads as deflection rather than flexibility. Everyone writes it. It tells the recruiter nothing except that you didn't want to answer.

Second, and more specific to this role: you're applying to **Marketing Data Insights**. The job is literally research, benchmarking and turning numbers into a recommendation. A one-line answer that shows you looked up the market and landed on a defensible range is a small, free demonstration of the thing they're hiring for. "Competitive" demonstrates the opposite.

"Market rate" is marginally better than "competitive" because it at least implies you checked. But it still makes the recruiter do the work of guessing what you think market is.

The cost of the better answer is about eight seconds:

```
$20-$25/hour, flexible and open to the program's standard rate.
```

Same flexibility, same zero risk, but now there's a number behind it.

One thing worth saying clearly: the fear that usually drives people to write "competitive" is anchoring too low and leaving money on the table. That fear doesn't apply here. Intern rates are fixed before the req posts. You cannot anchor yourself below the band, because the band isn't moving. So vagueness buys you no protection, which means there's no reason to pay its cost.

If you've already submitted it with "Competitive Salary," leave it. It's not worth withdrawing an application over. Just use the number version on the other five.

## 1. Personal Recruiting Overview

**Name:** Benjamin Jones

**Year & Major:** Second-year (sophomore), Bachelor of Science in Business Administration, Information Systems, UF Heavener School of Business. Expected graduation May 2029. GPA 3.8. Certificates in AI Fundamentals and Applications and Professional Selling. Starting the MS-ISOM combination degree with a Data Science concentration in Spring 2027.

**Target Industry/Industries:**
1. Technology / Tech Sales
2. Data & Analytics

**Reasoning for Path Choices:**

I picked these two because they are the two halves of the work I am already doing, and each one makes me better at the other.

On the sales side, I am a Tech Sales Analyst in the Florida Tech Pathways Analyst Program and I am pursuing the Professional Selling certificate at UF. At Volvera AI, the three-person consultancy I co-founded, I build client cold email programs end to end, from the prospect list through the sequences to the reply tracking, so I have run the top of a real funnel and watched which messages actually book meetings. Tech sales is where my ability to explain a technical product to someone who does not care about the technology turns into revenue.

On the data side, I am an Information Systems major heading into a Data Science concentration. At Global Source Data Solutions I built an automated campaign pipeline in n8n that cut launch time from a full day to under ten minutes, and I work across a catalog of 301M consumer records and 486M B2B contacts. That taught me that the interesting problem is usually the data underneath the campaign, not the campaign.

The reason I am recruiting for both rather than picking one is that the roles I want long term sit in the overlap: marketing analytics, revenue operations, go-to-market strategy. Every one of those jobs is held by someone who can sell and can query. I would rather build both muscles now than specialize early and have to backfill the other one later.

---

## 2. Recruiting Timeline Research

### Industry 1: Technology / Tech Sales

- **Earliest Applications Open:** September 2026 for large and mid-size tech. Gartner's North America Summer 2027 Sales Internship (Fort Myers, FL) posted in this window and is live now. Mid-size tech and SaaS open October through December 2026. Startups open January through March 2027.
- **Latest Applications Close:** Mostly rolling with no posted deadline. The window runs through March 2027, but roles are pulled as they fill, and the prior-cycle Gartner sales internship was removed in March after it filled.

### Industry 2: Data & Analytics

- **Earliest Applications Open:** July 2026 at big tech, where analytics roles post alongside software engineering (Amazon and Databricks in July and August, Microsoft and Apple in September, Meta September to October, Google mid-October for a two-to-four week window). Sophomore-specific programs open September 2026 through January 2027.
- **Latest Applications Close:** November 2026 through January 2027 for structured programs. Financial services analytics follows the parent industry and is mostly closed by December. Mid-size companies run rolling into March 2027.

### Key Observations from Research

**1. My binding constraint is graduation date, not GPA or experience.** This is the single most important thing I found. Most Summer 2027 postings require graduation within a year of the internship, which means December 2027 or May 2028. I graduate May 2029, so I am screened out of those regardless of how strong my resume is. Two concrete examples I verified:

- Gartner's Summer 2027 Sales Internship requires a December 2027 or May 2028 graduation date. I am not eligible, even though Fort Myers and the sales track fit me well. That one becomes a Summer 2028 target.
- Raymond James Summer Associate, Marketing Data Insights (R-0012995) explicitly allows "sophomore or junior standing" with a graduation date of "May 2028 or later." I am eligible.

The practical rule: read the graduation-date line before anything else on every posting. It decides eligibility faster than any other filter.

**2. My two industries have completely different structures for someone my year.** Data and analytics has roughly a dozen named programs built specifically for sophomores (Microsoft Explore, Google ASDI, NVIDIA Ignite, Capital One Early Internship Program, JPMorganChase Fellowship, Salesforce Futureforce Tech Launchpad, Bank of America Global Technology Summer Analyst, Wells Fargo Sophomore Experience, Palantir Accelerate, Zillow ZEAL, Visa Sophomore Internship Program, Dropbox Emerging Talent). Tech sales has essentially none. That means two different tactics: apply to named programs for data, and work req-by-req for sales at companies whose postings do not gate on class year.

**3. I am already inside the window, not ahead of it.** Several of those sophomore programs opened in September and close in October or November 2026. Palantir Accelerate opened in August. This is not a spring task.

**4. For rolling roles, the opening date is the real deadline.** Multiple sources say to apply within the first week or two of a posting because seats fill as the class fills, well before any published close date. Being early matters more than being polished.

**5. The tech sales path for a sophomore runs through mid-market, not enterprise.** Because sales internships are posted individually rather than as cohort programs, they rarely gate hard on class year. That makes Florida-based tech companies and mid-market SaaS my highest-probability route, and it is also where I have a geographic advantage.

**6. Some postings have hard requirements that are easy to miss.** Raymond James requires a cover letter naming Raymond James Marketing specifically, and it is listed under Educational Experience rather than anywhere obvious. Several sophomore programs also carry additional eligibility criteria beyond class year that I need to read individually.

---

### Company Preferences

Ranked, based on what would actually change my decision between two offers:

**1. Pay and location cost.** These are not the same thing and I am treating them as one line. A role in Miami, Fort Lauderdale, Tampa or St. Petersburg costs me nothing in housing, so a $22/hour role in St. Pete can beat a higher hourly rate in New York once summer sublet costs are in. This is the main reason my list is Florida-weighted rather than hub-weighted.

**2. Return offer and full-time conversion.** I graduate May 2029, which means Summer 2027 is my first real internship and Summer 2028 is the one that converts. I would rather spend 2027 at a company with a published conversion track record and a real shot at being invited back, so that 2028 is a decision instead of a search.

**3. Technical skill building.** I want a role where I am actually in SQL, Excel and a BI tool, not building decks about work other people did. The tie-breaker question I will ask every interviewer: what did last summer's intern on this team build, and is it still running?

Secondary, not decisive: team size (I would rather be the only intern on a small team than one of forty in a cohort), and whether the company does anything with AI tooling internally, since that is where most of my GSDSI and Volvera work has been.

---

## Two things worth knowing before you submit

**Gartner is the one real loss here.** It was tier 1 in your career expo briefing, the Fort Myers sales internship is live right now, and it fits you on every dimension except the one that matters. December 2027 or May 2028 graduation. Worth putting on a calendar for Summer 2028, when you will be exactly the grad year they want.

**Your tech sales column is thinner than your data column, and that's structural, not a research gap.** There is no sophomore pipeline in tech sales the way there is in data. If you want a tech sales internship specifically for Summer 2027, the realistic play is applying to individual mid-market SaaS reqs in Tampa, Miami and Fort Lauderdale as they post, starting now, and accepting a lower hit rate per application. If your program will let you, you could also argue for Data & Analytics as Industry 1 and tech sales as Industry 2, since that's where your odds actually are this cycle.

Most Summer 2027 postings require graduation within a year of the internship, meaning December 2027 or May 2028. I graduate May 2029, so I am filtered out before anyone reads my resume. Two verified examples:

- Gartner's North America Summer 2027 Sales Internship (Fort Myers, FL) explicitly requires a December 2027 or May 2028 graduation date. I am not eligible despite being a strong fit on every other dimension. It becomes a Summer 2028 target.
- Raymond James Summer Associate, Marketing Data Insights (R-0012995) explicitly allows "sophomore or junior standing" with a graduation date of "May 2028 or later." I am eligible.

The practical rule: read the graduation-date line before anything else on a posting. It decides eligibility faster than any other filter.

**2. Neither of my industries has a sophomore cohort program, and that shapes my entire strategy.** Research on marketing internships notes they are typically taken in the summer between junior and senior year, and tech sales has no sophomore-specific pipeline either. Finance and software have roughly a dozen named sophomore programs; my two industries have close to zero. That means I cannot run a program-application strategy. I have to work req-by-req, apply the week postings go live, and target companies whose postings do not gate on class year.

**3. I am already late for the earliest segment.** CPG and consumer brands opened in August and September 2026 and P&G fills cohorts by November. If I want a CPG brand internship I am applying in the back half of an open window right now, not getting ahead of one.

**4. Agencies are my longest runway and my least rigid option.** The agency window runs September 2026 through March 2027, with Omnicom and Dentsu into March and Wieden+Kennedy's deadline on April 1. Agencies also hire on portfolio and hustle more than class year. That makes them the right fallback if the fall corporate cycle does not land, and the reason I should not treat January as the end of my search.

**5. For rolling roles, the opening date is the real deadline.** Multiple sources say to apply within the first week or two of a posting, because seats fill as the class fills regardless of any published close date.

**6. Living in Florida is a structural advantage I had not accounted for.** South and Central Florida have an unusual density of corporate marketing headquarters: Raymond James in St. Petersburg, Chewy in Plantation, Royal Caribbean and Carnival in the Miami area, Restaurant Brands International in Miami, Bacardi in Coral Gables, Darden and Disney in Orlando, Office Depot in Boca Raton, Publix in Lakeland. These have smaller applicant pools than New York, and several are commutable from home, which removes summer housing cost from the equation entirely.

**7. Hard requirements are easy to miss.** Raymond James requires a cover letter naming Raymond James Marketing specifically, and it is buried under Educational Experience rather than listed with the application steps. Agency applications frequently require a portfolio or work samples, which I need to assemble before the window, not during it.

## Key Observations from Research

The biggest thing I learned is that my binding constraint is my graduation date, not my GPA or my experience. Most Summer 2027 postings require graduating within a year of the internship, which means December 2027 or May 2028, and I graduate May 2029. Gartner's Summer 2027 Sales Internship in Fort Myers states that requirement outright, so I am filtered out before anyone reads my resume even though the role fits me well. Raymond James goes the other way and explicitly allows sophomore or junior standing with a graduation date of May 2028 or later, which is why I am eligible there. The practical takeaway is that the graduation-date line decides my eligibility faster than anything else on a posting, so I read it first.

Neither of my industries has a sophomore cohort program, and that shapes everything else. Finance and software have roughly a dozen named programs built for second-year students, but marketing internships are typically taken between junior and senior year, and tech sales has no sophomore pipeline at all. So I cannot run a program-application strategy. I have to work posting by posting, apply in the first week or two a req goes live, and concentrate on companies whose postings do not gate on class year, which in practice means mid-market and regional employers rather than the big named programs.

On timing, I am inside the window rather than ahead of it. CPG and consumer brands opened in August and September 2026 and P&G fills its cohorts by November, so I am already applying into the back half of the earliest segment. Agencies are my longest runway, running September 2026 through March 2027, with Omnicom and Dentsu into March and Wieden+Kennedy's deadline on April 1. Agencies also hire more on portfolio and initiative than on class year, which makes them the right fallback if the fall corporate cycle does not land, and the reason I should not treat January as the end of my search. I do need to build a portfolio before that window rather than during it.

The last thing I found was an advantage I had not accounted for. South and Central Florida have an unusual density of corporate marketing headquarters: Raymond James in St. Petersburg, Chewy in Plantation, Royal Caribbean and Carnival near Miami, Restaurant Brands International in Miami, Bacardi in Coral Gables, Darden and Disney in Orlando, Office Depot in Boca Raton. These have smaller applicant pools than New York and several are commutable from home, which takes summer housing cost out of the decision entirely. I also need to watch for requirements buried in the posting body. Raymond James requires a cover letter naming Raymond James Marketing specifically, and it is listed under Educational Experience rather than with the application steps, which is exactly the kind of thing that gets an otherwise good application screened out.

### Claude · 2026-10-08 17:33

The thing that makes this field good instead of filler is ranking. Anyone can list culture, location and growth. What shows you've actually thought about it is putting them in order and naming the tradeoff you'd accept. Here's a version in your situation:

---

**Company Preferences**

Pay and location cost matter most, and I treat them as one factor rather than two. A role in St. Petersburg, Tampa or South Florida costs me nothing in housing, so $22 an hour near home can beat a higher rate in New York once a summer sublet is priced in. That is the main reason my target list is weighted toward Florida headquarters instead of the traditional hubs.

Second is whether the role leads somewhere. I graduate in May 2029, so Summer 2027 is my first real internship and Summer 2028 is the one that converts to full-time. I would rather spend 2027 somewhere with a real return-offer track record so that 2028 is a decision I get to make rather than another search I have to run.

Third is whether I would actually be in the systems. I want a role where I am in the email platform, the CRM, Excel and a BI tool, building things and then reading what they returned, not assembling decks about work other people did. I have already built that kind of work at a small company and at my own startup, so what I am buying with a summer is seeing how it is done at scale. My tie-breaker question for any interviewer is what last summer's intern on the team built, and whether it is still running.

What I am willing to trade away is brand name. I would take a hands-on role at a Florida company most people have not heard of over a prestigious one where I am the fortieth intern in a cohort.

### Claude · 2026-10-13 16:09

Congratulations, that's the one you were eligible for and the one that fit best. Your Spark Hire deadline is Friday, October 16.

Researching pay and the actual work now.

> *Claude used Agent: Research Raymond James intern pay*

> *Claude used Agent: Research RJ Marketing Data Insights work*

> *Claude used WebFetch: https://www.raymondjames.com/careers/all-access/campus-recruiting/intern-spotlight-marketing-2021*

> *Claude used WebSearch: Spark Hire one-way video interview Raymond James summer associate questions format*

> *Claude used WebFetch: https://www.glassdoor.com/Interview/Raymond-James-Financial-Summer-Internship-Interview-Questions-EI_IE559.0,23_KO24,41.htm*

Both research threads are back. Here's what I found, separating what Raymond James actually disclosed from what's crowdsourced.

## Is it paid? Yes.

Every corporate Raymond James early-careers posting is explicitly tagged **"Paid,"** classified as temporary, and capped at "up to 40 hours per week, Monday through Friday." It's an hourly wage, not a salary.

**The one employer-disclosed number** for your exact team: the 2025 Spring Associate posting for Marketing Data Insights in St. Petersburg stated **$23.00 per hour**. Same team, same office, one year earlier, part-time spring version of your role.

Everything else is crowdsourced, and it lines up:

| Source | Figure | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| RJ 2025 Spring Associate, Marketing Data Insights | **$23.00/hr** | Employer-disclosed |
| Glassdoor, "Summer Associate," 4 submissions, all St. Pete | $20, $22, $20–24, $21–25/hr | Self-reported, 2024–2025 |
| Indeed, Summer Associate | $24.87/hr | Estimated from postings |
| Indeed, RJ interns in Florida | $23.08/hr average, $15–34.85 range | From 7 postings |
| Glassdoor, St. Pete interns, all divisions | $18–$29/hr across 2023–2026 | Wide spread by division |

**Realistic expectation: $20 to $25 per hour.** Your program runs June 2 to August 13, 2027, which is about 10 weeks, so at 40 hours that's roughly **$8,000 to $10,000 for the summer**.

Three things to ignore. Glassdoor's headline "average $42/hr, $158,504/year" for Summer Associate is flatly contradicted by its own four submissions and is a broken algorithmic estimate. Levels.fyi's $32/hr figures are software engineering only. And one site claims $72,000–$95,000 annualized, which is unsourced and inflated by investment banking.

**No housing stipend.** Housing is confirmed only for software engineering ($1,575/month in 2022 and 2024, $3,500 in 2025) and claimed for investment banking ($4,000). Not one marketing, communications, or business posting mentions housing, relocation, or a signing bonus.

**But read your own email again:** the role is "St. Petersburg, FL **(Hybrid or Virtual)**." If virtual is genuinely on the table, your housing cost is zero and this becomes the best-paying option on your list by a wide margin, since you'd keep the full $8–10K. That is worth asking the recruiter directly once you're past the video round. Given that you ranked pay-relative-to-location-cost as your top factor, this is the single most important unknown in the whole process.

Two inconsistencies in their own posting, so don't be thrown: the text says "9-week immersive experience" but the dates give about 10.5 weeks, and "up to 40 hours" is a ceiling, not a promise. Marketing Associate roles at Raymond James are frequently part-time (the Spring versions run 20–24 hours), so confirm the weekly hours before you budget.

## What you'd actually be doing

You'd sit in **Marketing Insights & Technology**, which is one of four groups in Raymond James Marketing alongside Communications, Agency Services (their in-house creative agency), and Marketing Strategy & Branding.

Insights & Technology has three sub-teams, and their own posting says the intern completes assignments **across all three**:

1. **Research & Competitive Intelligence** — market research and competitor intelligence
2. **Data Driven Insights** — writing queries, building datasets, dashboards and reporting
3. **Data Driven Marketing** — campaign-side analytics and marketing technology

The 2024 version of this exact internship listed the duties concretely:

- Write data queries, build datasets, and analyze data
- Design and build reports and data visualizations
- Modify and optimize marketing technology, reporting, and tools
- Communicate findings to key stakeholders
- Conduct market research and gather competitor intelligence
- Support multi-stage projects

Your 2027 posting adds "complete projects related to branding, content creation, and social media marketing," so it is not purely quantitative. Expect a mix.

**The stack:** SQL, Tableau, SSRS, Excel and PowerPoint for analysis and reporting. Campaign execution runs on Salesforce and Salesforce Marketing Cloud (Email Studio, Journey Builder, Automation Studio, AMPscript). Their roughly 8,000 financial advisor websites run on Sitecore. For the intern role, **SQL and Tableau are listed as "preferred," not required.** The only hard requirements are Excel and PowerPoint.

I specifically checked for and did **not** find Adobe Analytics, Adobe Experience Manager, Snowflake, Python, R, or Qualtrics anywhere in their marketing org. Don't prepare for those.

**The job this converts into** is Data Driven Insights Analyst, and that posting states the technical bar with unusual precision: write SQL queries using JOINs and aggregations (COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX, ORDER BY), and build interactive Tableau dashboards with filters. That is a very reachable bar, and it tells you exactly what to learn between now and June.

**Who the work is for:** this is heavily B2B. The internal customers are Raymond James' own financial advisors and business units, not retail consumers. Their consumer brand platform is "Life Well Planned," and their 2024 campaign targeted a named audience segment they call "Entrusters," affluent clients near retirement. A proprietary behavioral segment like that is exactly the kind of output this team produces, and it is the clearest public trace of their work showing up in firm strategy.

Marketing reports up through COO Scott Curtis, who becomes Bank Segment President on January 1, 2027. The CMO is Mike White, though my most recent hard confirmation of him in that seat is March 2024 and he isn't on the current public leadership page, so verify on LinkedIn before you mention anyone by name.

## The program itself

Nine to ten weeks, hybrid out of the St. Petersburg office at 880 Carillon Pkwy. Cohort induction week, ongoing training including presentation and networking workshops, executive fireside chats with C-suite leaders, an assigned peer coach, shadowing, community service days, and an intern day at a Rays game.

On return offers, their internships page says high performers are identified before the summer ends and "most interns who receive full-time offers return after graduation." That matters given you ranked conversion second.

## Why you're actually a fit, and the one gap

The overlap is better than you might realize. Campaign effectiveness measurement is the core of this team's work, and at Volvera you built reply tracking specifically to see which sequence version books meetings. That is the same question. At GSDSI you built dashboards, ran campaigns to 3,000+ prospects, and maintained CRM data hygiene. The one thing on their list you can't yet claim is SQL and Tableau, and those are preferred rather than required.

## On the Spark Hire round, due Friday

It's a one-way recorded interview, no live person. From Glassdoor, a 2025 candidate who got an offer did Spark Hire first, then a Zoom round focused on role-specific technical questions one to two weeks later, with an offer about two weeks after that. Reported questions were an introduction, why Raymond James, why this field, why you're the best fit, and how you handle stress. Small sample, two reviews.

Want me to build you a prep kit for it? I'd script answers to the likely questions in your voice using the GSDSI and Volvera material, pull in the "Life Well Planned" and Entrusters context so you sound like you did homework, and give you a setup checklist. You have three days and the recording is mandatory.

Sources: [Raymond James internships program page](https://www.raymondjames.com/careers/students-and-recent-grads/internships) · [2024 Marketing Data Insights Intern posting](https://www.wayup.com/i-Financial-Services-j-2024-Summer-Internship-Program-Marketing-Data-Insights-Intern-St-Petersburg-FL-Raymond-James-508001680250987/) · [2025 Spring Associate, Marketing Data Insights, $23/hr](https://www.wayup.com/i-Financial-Services-j-2025-Spring-Associate-Marketing-Data-Insights-St-Petersburg-FL-Virtual-or-Hybrid-Raymond-James-172430373234626/) · [Glassdoor Summer Associate submissions](https://www.glassdoor.com/Salary/Raymond-James-Financial-Summer-Associate-Salaries-E559_D_KO24,40.htm) · [Indeed RJ intern pay, Florida](https://www.indeed.com/cmp/Raymond-James/salaries/Intern/Florida) · [Data Driven Insights Analyst posting](https://www.career.com/job/raymond-james-financial-inc/data-driven-insights-analyst/j202209171518102303670) · [Salesforce Marketing Cloud Administrator posting](https://builtin.com/job/salesforce-marketing-cloud-administrator/7322693) · [Glassdoor RJ summer internship interviews](https://www.glassdoor.com/Interview/Raymond-James-Financial-Summer-Internship-Interview-Questions-EI_IE559.0,23_KO24,41.htm) · [MediaPost on the 2024 brand campaign](https://www.mediapost.com/publications/article/394081/raymond-james-plans-for-life-in-3-new-spots.html) · [Sept 2026 executive appointments](https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/09/15/3362656/0/en/raymond-james-financial-announces-key-executive-leadership-appointments.html)

Under 30 minutes. Researching now, and there's one thing you need to hear before the call.

> *Claude used WebFetch: https://renew-ap.com/*

> *Claude used WebSearch: "Adam Plager" QKSS*

> *Claude used WebSearch: "Renewable Asset Partners" Stephen Dictor Chicago renewable energy data*

## Read this first

**Adam is not an outside referrer. He works there.** His site bio lists him as Lead Data Engineer at Renewable Asset Partners (named Head of Engineering elsewhere on the page), alongside a 25-year career in energy trading, risk management and petroleum engineering. "A friend of mine at RAP is hiring" is him being casual. This is a screening call with the technical lead, not a networking chat. Treat it that way.

**The SQL thing.** Adam opened with "especially your SQL work." On your resume, SQL appears in exactly one place: the Florida Tech Pathways bullet, which is the program's template language, and SQL isn't in your Technical Skills line at all. If he asks you to walk through your SQL, do not bluff. A 25-year data engineer will know inside of two questions.

The honest version is also a good answer: *"Most of my pipeline work has been at the automation and orchestration layer rather than hand-written SQL. Here's what I actually built."* Then go to the n8n pipeline. Say it that way and you come off as someone who knows the edges of what he knows, which is the single most valuable trait in a junior data hire at a 5-person firm. Pretend otherwise and you lose the referral and Jared's credibility with it.

Same note on the "Professional Selling minor." It's a certificate. Don't correct him theatrically, just use the right word yourself.

## What the company is

Chicago, founded 2018, roughly 2 to 10 people. They describe themselves as uniting energy experts and technology practitioners to give impartial recommendations, meaning they aren't reselling anyone's software.

**Four services:** data analytics launch (analysis-ready data, automation platforms, database support), condition monitoring (tuning asset monitoring systems), investment due diligence (independent technical assessment of raw wind and solar operational data when someone is acquiring a portfolio), and analytics team training.

**What they build:** a SQL database for asset information and metadata with a custom web interface, self-service dashboards pitched as an alternative to paying for Power BI or Tableau licenses, automated delivery of recurring reports and weather forecasts, and custom tools for warranties, supplier performance and KPIs.

**Stephen Dictor**, Founder and Principal. 15+ years in renewables, analytics and software. Asset performance management, supply and operations agreement negotiation, analytics software deployment. Rice University and IESE Business School.

## Your three stories, mapped to their services

Don't recite your resume. Pick these:

**The n8n pipeline.** You cut campaign launch from a full day to under ten minutes. RAP sells "automated delivery of recurring reports." That is the same muscle: taking a manual recurring process and making it run itself. Lead with this one.

**Scale.** The 14-product catalog across 301M consumer records and 486M B2B contacts. They work with wind and solar operational data, which is high-volume time series. You've worked with data big enough that you can't eyeball it.

**Volvera's reply tracking.** You built the measurement layer that shows which sequence version actually books meetings. That is both halves of this job at once: you built the instrumentation, and you built it to answer a sales question. It's also your best evidence for the technical sales half, because you've done outbound for a tiny consultancy, which is exactly what RAP is.

## Questions to ask

Lead with this one, early, not at the end:

**"Junior Data Analyst sounds like a full-time role. I'm a sophomore graduating May 2029. Is there a version of this that works part-time during the school year, or is it summer-shaped?"** Get it out in the first five minutes. A five-person firm can be flexible where a formal program can't, but you don't want to spend fifteen minutes impressing him and then reveal a dealbreaker.

Then:

- The role is described as half data engineering, half technical sales. What does the sales half actually look like day to day, is it outbound, scoping calls with existing clients, or supporting Stephen on proposals?
- Your site mentions self-service dashboards as an alternative to Power BI and Tableau licenses. Is that a product you've built once, or rebuilt per client?
- What's the stack? Is it SQL plus Python, or something else?
- The investment due diligence work, assessing raw wind and solar data for portfolio acquisitions, sounds like the most interesting thing on the site. Would someone junior touch that?
- Is the team in Chicago, or is this remote-friendly?
- What made you reach out after reading the resume?

That last one is free intelligence. Whatever he says is the thing to emphasize for the rest of the call and in the intro to Stephen.

## Logistics

Quiet room, pull up renew-ap.com in a tab, have your resume open so you can see your own bullets. It's 15 minutes, so he's deciding one thing: do I put my name behind this kid with Stephen. Be concrete, admit the edges of what you know, and ask about the schedule constraint up front.

Good luck. Tell me how it goes and I'll help with the follow-up email.

### Claude · 2026-10-13 22:07

Don't say "operations" alone — it's too broad and a CEO will file you under supply chain. Narrow it to the specific function and give him two or three real titles he can pattern-match against people in his network.

## The titles to name

**1. Revenue Operations / Marketing Operations intern.** This is your actual sweet spot and almost nobody your age knows the title exists. RevOps is the person who builds and owns the systems a sales team runs on: the CRM, the sequences, the dashboards, the attribution. That is literally what you do at GSDSI and what you own at Volvera. It exists at nearly every SaaS company and it's chronically understaffed, which means less competition.

**2. Sales Development Representative (SDR) or Tech Sales intern.** Adam already flagged this, so keep it. It's the highest-volume category and the Professional Selling certificate backs it.

**3. Marketing Analytics or Business Analyst intern (go-to-market side).** This is what the Raymond James role is. Name it so he knows you can do the quantitative version too.

If he wants a fourth, **Sales Engineer / Solutions Engineer intern** is worth saying, because the RAP role is essentially that and it tells him you're comfortable being the technical person in a client meeting.

## The one line he can forward

Give him something he can paste into an email, because that's what he's actually going to do:

> Benjamin is a UF Information Systems sophomore who builds the systems sales and marketing teams run on. He automated a campaign pipeline at a data company this summer that cut launch time from a full day to under ten minutes, and he co-founded a consultancy where he builds clients' outbound email, CRM and landing pages. He's comfortable on both sides: he can build the pipeline and he can talk to the customer. Looking for Summer 2027.

That's the whole pitch. The reason it works is that it names a shape, not a job title, and lets whoever reads it decide which of their open roles fits.

## The three constraints he needs to know

Tell him these unprompted, because leaving them out wastes his referrals:

**Graduation is May 2029.** This is the big one. Most Summer 2027 postings require graduating by May 2028, and you get auto-screened out. If Adam sends you to three rising-senior-only roles, he burns three favors and concludes you're not landing anywhere. Tell him you need roles open to sophomores or rising juniors, and that it's a hard filter, not a preference.

**Timing:** Summer 2027 is the target, and you're available part-time during the school year now if something's remote.

**Geography:** Florida and the East Coast, or remote. Say you're flexible on remote, because for a small company that removes their biggest objection.

## One more thing

Mention you're already in process at Raymond James for a Summer Associate role in Marketing Data Insights and are at the video interview stage. Two reasons. It tells him you're a real candidate other people are moving on, which makes him more willing to spend his name on you. And it tells him the exact flavor of role that's already working, so his referrals aim at the same target instead of scattering.

Keep the ask tight. He offered, so give him a short forwardable paragraph and three titles, not a list of ten. People refer the candidates who are easy to describe.

### Claude · 2026-10-15 14:27

Before the answers: skip the buzzwords. A recruiter is watching dozens of these recordings back to back, and "leverage," "synergy" and "data-driven" are exactly what makes them blur together. Specificity is the only thing that survives. One real number from your own work beats a paragraph of polish.

Let me verify a couple of Raymond James facts so you can say something nobody else will.

> *Claude used WebSearch: Raymond James consecutive profitable quarters independence founded 1962 Bob James core values*

> *Claude used WebFetch: https://www.raymondjames.com/about-us/company-history*

> *Claude used WebFetch: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_James_Financial*

## Facts to have in your head

Founded 1962 by Robert James in St. Petersburg, merged with Edward Raymond's firm in 1964 to become Raymond James & Associates. Still headquartered in St. Pete. Roughly 8,700 financial advisors, over $1 trillion in client assets, and **more than 150 consecutive profitable quarters**, which is nearly 40 years without a losing quarter.

**Core values:** client-first, integrity, independence, conservative long-term view.
**Five guiding behaviors:** Develop, Collaborate, Decide, Deliver, Improve.

Don't recite those. Let one show up inside another answer.

---

## Why Raymond James

> Two things. The first is independence. Raymond James isn't owned by a bank conglomerate, and the firm has been profitable for more than 150 consecutive quarters, which is almost forty years without a losing one. That conservative, long-term view shows up in the marketing. "Life Well Planned" isn't selling returns, it's selling a relationship that lasts decades. For someone who wants to work in marketing data, that's a harder and more interesting problem, because you're measuring trust over years instead of clicks over a week.
>
> The second is Florida. I'm at UF, I'm from Miami, and Bob James started this firm in St. Petersburg in 1962. I'd rather build something at a firm with roots in my state than chase a name somewhere else.

## Why this role / what stands out about it

> What stands out is that this intern rotates across all three teams inside Marketing Insights and Technology: research and competitive intelligence, data-driven insights, and data-driven marketing. Most analyst internships put you in one lane. This one lets me see the whole path, from what's the question, to here's the dashboard, to here's what we actually changed about the campaign.
>
> The other thing is who the customer is. Marketing here serves your own advisors, around 8,700 of them, so it's B2B marketing to a network rather than consumer brand work. That's closer to what I've actually done. This summer I built an automated campaign pipeline that took launch time from a full day to under ten minutes, and the valuable part wasn't the automation, it was finally having clean data on which sequences worked. That's the question this team gets paid to answer, just at a much bigger scale.

## Strengths

> The main one is that I build things instead of recommending them. This summer I didn't write a memo about our campaign process being slow, I built the pipeline that fixed it. Launch went from a full day to under ten minutes and it's still running. Same at the consultancy I co-founded, where I built a review system that texts a customer a one-tap review link the minute their job is marked complete.
>
> The second is translating between technical and non-technical people. I'm an Information Systems major so I'm comfortable in the data, but I'm also Assistant Comptroller on the ACCENT executive board, where I have to explain financial policy to students who just want their event funded. The analyst job isn't only building the dashboard, it's getting someone to act on what it says.

## Weakness

This is the one that decides the interview. Give a real one.

> My SQL isn't as deep as I want it to be yet. Most of my pipeline and dashboard work has been at the automation and tooling layer, n8n and APIs and no-code platforms, rather than writing queries by hand. I could give you a softer answer, but you'd find out in week one.
>
> What I'm doing about it: I looked at your Data Driven Insights Analyst posting, which is the full-time version of this seat, and it names the actual bar. Joins, aggregations, and building interactive Tableau dashboards with filters. That's specific enough to practice against, so that's what I'm working through this semester, and I start UF's data science concentration in the spring. If I'm there in June I want to be past the learning curve, not starting it.

Two reasons this works. It's true, so you'll never get caught. And referencing their full-time job posting proves you did research nobody else did.

Avoid "I'm a perfectionist," "I work too hard," and "I take on too much." Those read as evasions.

## Conflict with a teammate

I'm not going to write this one for you, because a fabricated conflict story falls apart on the first follow-up question and the whole point is that it sounds like it happened to you. Pick a real one and build it out. Your strongest options:

**ACCENT, Assistant Comptroller.** You process reimbursements under the Student Government fiscal code, which means sometimes you tell a peer no. That's structural conflict with someone who wants something from you. It's also the best fit for Raymond James, because marketing at a financial firm goes through Legal and Compliance review constantly, and they want someone who can hold a line politely.

**Volvera.** Three co-founders, no manager, no org chart. Any disagreement about scope, direction or who owns what is a real conflict story.

**Homecoming.** Ten-plus cross-functional teams shipping website updates on deadline. Someone wanted a change that broke something, or missed a handoff.

Structure it in four beats, about 60 seconds total: what the disagreement actually was, what you specifically did (talked to them directly, not around them), how it resolved, and what you'd do differently. Land on something you'd change. The point of the question is whether you can hold a conflict without making it personal, and whether you learn.

The one thing to avoid is a story where the other person is simply wrong and you were right the whole time.

## Delivery

Spark Hire is recorded with no interviewer, so there's no one to read the room for you. Record standing up if you can. Look at the camera lens, not your own face on the screen. Plain wall behind you, light in front of you not behind you. Aim for 60 to 90 seconds per answer, which is enough for one real example and no more.

Have one number ready for every answer. "Under ten minutes," "3,000 prospects," "8,700 advisors," "150 consecutive quarters." Numbers are what the reviewer remembers four recordings later, and they're the opposite of buzzwords.

Last thing: they require a cover letter naming Raymond James Marketing specifically. Make sure that actually got attached before the deadline tomorrow.

### Claude · 2026-10-15 14:50

> Honestly, what got me was realizing that independence changes what the marketing job actually is here. Raymond James doesn't own its advisors the way a wirehouse does. Close to 8,700 of them choose to affiliate, and they can leave. So marketing can't just push things out and assume they get used. It has to earn that. Which means somebody has to actually know what's working, and that's the team I'd be sitting on.
>
> The second thing is the time horizon. More than 150 consecutive profitable quarters is almost forty years without a losing one, and you can see that same patience in the brand. "Life Well Planned" isn't selling a product or a return, it's selling a relationship that outlasts whatever the market did last quarter. Measuring something like that is a harder analytics problem than measuring clicks, and that's the kind of problem I want to work on.
>
> And it's home. I'm at UF, I'm from Miami, and the firm has been in St. Petersburg since Bob James started it in 1962. I'd rather build something at a firm with roots in my state than go somewhere for the name on the building.

About 80 seconds out loud.

Three things to protect when you deliver it. Never say the word "values," and never say "independence, integrity, client-first" as a list. The answer already demonstrates three of them, and naming them out loud is what makes people sound like they memorized a careers page. Same with the five behaviors. Don't add "I'm excited about the opportunity to collaborate and deliver." Saying the word is the tell.

The first paragraph is the one that will land, because almost nobody connects independence to what it means for a marketing team. If you only remember one chunk, remember that one.

If you need a ten-second version because you're running out of time on the recording: *Independence means marketing here has to earn its use with 8,700 advisors who could walk, so knowing what actually works matters more than it would somewhere else. That's the job I want, and the firm has been in St. Pete since 1962, which is home for me.*

**Mission:** "Our business is people and their financial wellbeing." Committed to helping individuals, corporations and institutions achieve their unique goals, while developing successful professionals and helping communities prosper.

**Vision:** "To be a financial services firm as unique as the people we serve, transforming lives, businesses and communities through the power of personal relationships and professional advice."

**The four core values**, with how the firm words them:

- **We put clients first.** "If we do what's right for our clients, the firm will do well and we'll all benefit."
- **We act with integrity.** "We put others above self, and what's right above what's easy."
- **We think long term.** "We act responsibly, taking a conservative approach that translates into a strong, stable firm."
- **We value independence.** "We respect autonomy, celebrate individuality and welcome diverse perspectives."

**The five guiding behaviors:** Develop, Collaborate, Decide, Deliver, Improve.

One sourcing note so you don't get caught out: the values and the five behaviors are verbatim from the job description you were sent, so those are solid. The mission and vision wording above is from the Raymond James site, specifically the page I could render, which was their UK company page. The language is consistent with how the firm talks everywhere, but if you want to quote it word for word on camera, pull it up on raymondjames.com yourself first.

How to actually use this: don't recite any of it. The "why Raymond James" answer I gave you already demonstrates three of the four values without naming one. If you want a single thread to pull on, "we think long term" is the one that connects to everything else you're saying: 150-plus profitable quarters, a brand built on a relationship rather than a return, and an analytics problem that's about measuring trust over years. That's the throughline. Saying the words out loud is what breaks it.

### Claude · 2026-10-15 15:26

> *Claude used Bash: mkdir -p /home/claude/rj_interview && cp "/root/.claude/uploads/b1a88923-0073-54e3-a8cc-09ffab78ef28/64297c53-attachment.txt" /home/claude/rj_interview/khriz_mock_interview_transcript_2026-10-15.txt &...*

Saved. Here's what I pulled out of it that's useful, plus one thing you need to fix before you record.

## The number doesn't match your resume

In the call you said the pipeline took campaign setup **"from like an hour to 5 minutes."** Your resume says **"from a full day to under 10 minutes."**

Those are very different claims, and if a recruiter has your resume open while watching the recording, that's the kind of gap that creates doubt about everything else. Pick the true one and use only that one, everywhere.

Also worth noting: you told Khriz the stack was **n8n, Supabase, Instantly, and Lovable**. Your resume says "n8n, Instantly, Slack, Anthropic API." Supabase isn't on the resume and Slack wasn't in your spoken version. Get one accurate list in your head.

## What I now have that I didn't before

**Your conflict story.** Starting Volvera, Lee wanted to begin cold outreach immediately, before you had an LLC, a domain, business emails or a website. You and Adi pushed back that you needed to be a real entity first, both for legal exposure and because outreach from a personal address kills credibility. You went back and forth, and it resolved.

One thing I need you to confirm: at the end you said *"I'm gonna admit, I was wrong, you guys were right."* Was that Lee conceding to you and Adi, or you conceding to Lee? It completely changes the story. If Lee conceded, you need to add a line about what you learned anyway, or it reads as "I was right and he came around," which is the version interviewers dislike. If you conceded, that's actually the stronger story and you should lead with it.

**Your weakness.** You jump in before planning. On the pipeline you didn't learn the platforms first, you had Claude guide you as you went, you hit avoidable problems, and it took longer than it should have. That's honest and real, and it's better than the SQL version I wrote you earlier because it comes with a concrete example.

**New context:** Lee is the third Volvera co-founder, you have an FTP capstone running, and Adam Plager said give him three to four weeks.

## One flag on "thrive in ambiguity"

Khriz is right that it's a quality companies look for, but the phrase itself is heavily worn in recruiting. If you say it to someone who screens a lot of students, it registers as a thing you read somewhere. Describe the behavior instead: *"When I'm handed something with no instructions, I'd rather build a rough version and show it to someone than wait to be told what to do."* Same idea, sounds like you.

Want me to write the conflict and weakness answers out properly now that I have the real material? Tell me who conceded in the Volvera story and I'll do both.

### Claude · 2026-10-15 15:30

Built from your notes and Khriz's framing. Nothing invented. Two placeholders marked where I need a number from you, and clarifying questions at the bottom.

One fix first: Raymond James' actual mission is **"Our business is people and their financial wellbeing."** It doesn't say "treating businesses as people." That's your read on it, and it's a good one, but say it as your interpretation instead of quoting it that way. A marketing team will know their own mission statement.

---

## Why Raymond James

> I've wanted to do marketing research for a while, and this summer I actually got to do it at a small data company. The part I liked most was bringing a product to life, taking something that already existed internally and making other businesses understand why it mattered to them.
>
> What makes me want to do that at Raymond James specifically is the mission. It's "our business is people and their financial wellbeing." In an industry where most of the competition leads with products and performance, leading with people is genuinely unusual.
>
> And the part that matters for marketing is that I don't think it stops at individual clients. B2B marketing is usually the least personal marketing there is. You're selling to a logo. But if the firm's whole premise is people and their wellbeing, then the businesses and the advisors you're marketing to get treated the same way, as people with something at stake rather than accounts. That's the kind of marketing I want to do, and it's the reason I'd want to do it here rather than anywhere else.

About 65 seconds.

## Why marketing / why this role

> I'm interested in marketing because of what happens when it works. This summer I was the one writing and sending the campaigns, and the best part was hearing people respond to words I wrote. Watching someone go from not knowing who we were, to being interested, to wanting the product, because of how I positioned it.
>
> The other thing I like is that marketing is a continuous cycle. You put something out, you test it, you find out what worked and what didn't, and you run it again better. Seeing that whole process go from a cold email all the way to a signed contract showed me how much of the business sits at the top of the funnel. And that constant iterating is honestly a quality I see in myself.
>
> At Raymond James specifically, it's the scale. I've done this at a nine-person data company and at the startup I co-founded, where you're figuring it out with no budget and no team. I want to see what it looks like with real scale behind it, and I want to be around people further along than me who will tell me when I'm doing it wrong.

About 70 seconds.

## Strengths (STAR)

> My biggest strength is figuring out what to build when nobody tells me what to build.
>
> This summer my job was running email campaigns, and we were doing it in a platform called Instantly. Every single campaign meant manually entering all the information, building the list, and sending it, and it took about **[TIME A]** each time. Nobody asked me to fix that. That was just the process.
>
> So I automated it. I used n8n, **[TOOLS]** and Lovable, and I hadn't touched most of them before. I learned them while I was building and combined them into one pipeline with a dashboard on top.
>
> Campaign setup went from about **[TIME A]** to about **[TIME B]**, and we went from running one at a time to two or three a day. It's still running.
>
> The reason that's the story I'd pick is that nobody handed me the problem or the tools. I'd rather build a rough version and show it to someone than wait to be told what to do.

On "thrive in ambiguity": Khriz is right that it's a quality firms look for, but the phrase itself is worn out with anyone who screens a lot of students. The version above says the same thing in your own words, which is harder to dismiss. Your call.

## Weakness (STAR)

> Same project, opposite side of it. I jump in too fast.
>
> Building that pipeline, I didn't take the time to learn the platforms first. I just started, and I leaned on Claude to guide me as I went. That caught up with me. I ran into problems I would have seen coming if I'd spent an hour up front understanding how the tools connected end to end, and the build took longer than it should have.
>
> What I changed is that I don't lean on one tool anymore. I run the same problem through a couple of different models so I'm not treating one answer as the answer, I do real research before I start, and I give myself time to brainstorm instead of treating planning as the thing slowing me down. The biggest change is asking people who have built something similar before I begin. At a company that's free information and I wasn't using it.
>
> **[OPTIONAL: I'm doing it that way right now on my capstone for the Florida Tech Pathways analyst program.]**

## Conflict (STAR)

> When my two co-founders and I were starting Volvera, Ethan was eager to start cold outreach immediately so we could land our first clients early. He wanted to move, and I understood that, because I wanted it too.
>
> But we weren't set up. No LLC, and no domain, which meant any email we sent came from a personal address with nothing tying it to the business. My concern was two things. There was legal exposure in operating before we were an entity. And reaching out from a personal address would undercut us with the exact people we were trying to win, because they'd have no idea who we were or what service we provided and nothing to remember us by.
>
> We went back and forth. The way I handled it was to open with the fact that I was just as eager as he was, so it didn't land as me slowing things down. Then I made the case on execution rather than on caution. We only get one first impression with each prospect and I didn't want to spend it looking like three students with Gmail accounts. He agreed, and we held off until the entity and the domain were done.
>
> It was the right call for the business. Ethan is running outreach now off a real domain and we're close to our first paying clients. The thing I'd do differently is give him a date up front. I made the case for waiting but I never said waiting until when, and that's what turned it into a back-and-forth instead of a decision.

That last line is what keeps this from reading as "I was right and he came around," which is the version interviewers mark down.

## Six things I need from you

1. **The time number.** Your resume says the pipeline cut launch from a full day to under 10 minutes. You told Khriz an hour to 5 minutes. Which is true? Use the same one everywhere, including on the resume.

2. **The tool list.** You told Khriz n8n, Supabase, Instantly, Lovable. Your resume says n8n, Instantly, Slack, Anthropic API. What was actually in it?

3. **The capstone line.** Has your FTP capstone actually started, and are you really doing the plan-first thing on it? If not I'll cut that sentence. Don't say it if it isn't happening yet.

4. **The timeline in the conflict story.** I wrote your takeaway as "I should have given him a date." Is that accurate, or did you actually give him one? If you did, tell me and I'll swap in a different takeaway.

5. **Volvera headcount.** Three of you: you, Adi, and Ethan Leeds? And is "close to our first paying clients" still true as of today?

6. **"Thrive in ambiguity."** Keep the phrase or use the plain-English version I wrote?

Answer those and I'll lock the final versions. You're recording tomorrow at the latest, so practice these out loud three or four times each today. Khriz is right that it's how you say it, not what you say, and reading these cold on camera will sound read.

> I'm interested in marketing because of what happens when it works. This summer I was the one writing and sending the campaigns, and the best part was hearing people respond to words I wrote. Watching someone go from not knowing who we were, to being interested, to wanting the product, because of how I positioned it.
>
> The other thing I like is that marketing is a continuous cycle. You put something out, you test it, you find out what worked and what didn't, and you run it again better. Seeing that whole process go from a cold email all the way to a signed contract showed me how much of the business sits at the top of the funnel. That constant iterating is a quality I see in myself.
>
> At Raymond James specifically, it's the scale. I've done this at a small data company and at the startup I co-founded, where you're figuring it out with no budget and no team. I want to see what it looks like with real scale behind it, and I want to be around people further along than me who will tell me when I'm doing it wrong.

## Strength (STAR)

> My biggest strength is that I thrive in ambiguity. I'm good at figuring out what to build when nobody tells me what to build.
>
> This summer my job was running email campaigns, and we were doing all of it in a platform called Instantly. Every campaign meant manually entering the information, building the list, and sending it out, and start to finish that took most of a day. Nobody asked me to fix it. That was just the process.
>
> So I automated it. I used n8n, Instantly, Slack and the Anthropic API, and I hadn't worked with most of that before. I learned it while I was building and combined it into one pipeline with a dashboard on top.
>
> Campaign launch went from a full day to under ten minutes, and we went from running one at a time to two or three a day. It's still running.
>
> That's the story I'd pick because nobody handed me the problem or the tools. I'd rather build a rough version and show it to someone than wait to be told what to do.

## Weakness (STAR)

> Same project, opposite side of it. I jump in too fast.
>
> Building that pipeline, I didn't take the time to learn the platforms first. I just started, and I leaned on Claude to guide me as I went. That caught up with me. I ran into problems I would have seen coming if I'd spent an hour up front understanding how the tools connected end to end, and the build took longer than it should have.
>
> What I changed is that I don't lean on one tool anymore. I run the same problem through a couple of different models so I'm not treating one answer as the answer, I do real research before I start, and I give myself time to brainstorm instead of treating planning as the thing slowing me down. The biggest change is asking people who've built something similar before I begin, because at a company that's free information and I wasn't using it.
>
> I'm doing it that way right now on my capstone for the Florida Tech Pathways analyst program.

## Conflict (STAR)

> When my two co-founders and I were starting Volvera, Ethan was eager to start cold outreach immediately so we could land our first clients early. He wanted to move, and I understood that, because I wanted it too.
>
> But we weren't set up. No LLC, and no domain, which meant any email we sent came from a personal address with nothing tying it to the business. My concern was two things. There was real legal exposure in operating before we were an entity. And reaching out from a personal address would undercut us with the exact people we were trying to win, because they'd have no idea who we were or what service we provided and nothing to remember us by.
>
> We went back and forth on it. The way I approached it was to lead with the fact that I was just as eager as he was, so it wouldn't land as me slowing things down. He agreed, and we held off until the entity and the domain were done.
>
> It was the right call. Ethan is running outreach now off a real domain and we're close to our first paying clients. What I took from it is that the disagreement didn't actually move until I stopped arguing about legal risk and started arguing about execution. Once it was "we get one first impression with each prospect and I don't want to spend it looking like three students with Gmail accounts," we were on the same side of the problem instead of opposite sides.

One last thing. Your resume says "a full day to under 10 minutes," which is what these answers now say. If the real number is closer to an hour, change both. Say the same number on camera that's on the page in front of them.

Practice each one out loud three times before you record. Not in your head, out loud. The first time will be clunky and that's the point.

### Claude · 2026-10-15 16:39

Highest-return thing you can do, and the thing almost everyone skips: **record yourself on your phone and watch it back.** Not practice in your head, not read it over. Record, watch, cringe, fix, record again. Three cycles per answer and you'll be better than 90% of the people submitting.

## Before you practice: check the invite

Open the Spark Hire link and look at the setup screen before you record anything. You need to know:

- **How many takes per question.** Could be one, could be three. Completely changes how much pressure each attempt carries.
- **Prep time per question.** Usually around 30 seconds.
- **Time limit per answer.** If they cap you at 60 seconds, your current answers are too long and you need to cut.
- **Whether there's a practice question.** There usually is. Use it to check your audio, lighting and framing, not to rehearse.

Don't skip this. People find out mid-recording they only had one take.

## Today

**Round 1, out loud, alone, ~20 min.** Go through each of the five bullet sets and just talk. It'll be bad. Doesn't matter. The point is finding where you stall.

**Round 2, recorded, ~30 min.** Phone propped up, record each answer, watch it back immediately. You're looking for three things only:
- Did I land the number? (full day → under 10 minutes)
- Did I ramble past 90 seconds?
- How many times did I say "like" or "um"?

**Round 3, recorded, ~20 min.** Same five, fixing what you saw. Stop after this. More than three passes and you start sounding memorized, which is worse than sounding rough.

**Then a cold run.** Have someone throw you a question you haven't prepped. This matters because Spark Hire will likely ask something not on Khriz's list, and if your first unscripted moment happens on the real recording, it'll show.

## The question you haven't prepped

**"Tell me about yourself."** It's the most likely opener on any one-way interview and it's not in your bullets. Build it now, 45 seconds max, in this order:

- Second-year Information Systems at UF, Heavener
- This summer: marketing and data operations at a data company, built the campaign pipeline
- Also co-founded a three-person consultancy where I run the marketing side
- Starting a data science concentration in the spring
- Which is why marketing data at Raymond James is exactly the thing I want to spend a summer on

Present → recent past → where it's pointing. Ends on them.

Other ones worth having a rough shape for: a time you failed, a time you had to learn something fast, a time you worked on a team. All three can pull from the pipeline build or Volvera.

## Setup

- Camera at **eye level**. Books under the laptop. If you're on your phone, prop it, don't hold it.
- **Light in front of you**, not behind. Face a window. Never sit with one at your back.
- Plain wall. Make your bed if it's in frame.
- Wired earbuds with a mic beat a laptop mic.
- Do Not Disturb on everything. Tell whoever you live with.
- Water within reach.
- **Put your bullets on a sticky note right next to the camera lens**, four or five words per answer, not on the screen below it. If your notes are at the bottom of the screen, every glance down reads as looking away.
- Look **at the lens**, not at your own face. Your instinct will be to watch yourself. Don't.

## While recording

- The first 10 seconds of each answer is where you'll freeze, so that's the part to have cold. Know your opening line for all five.
- Start with a breath. One second of silence is invisible on playback and feels like ten to you.
- If you stumble mid-answer, keep going. Don't restart unless you have takes to burn. A small stumble reads as human. A restart burns an attempt.
- Say the number. Every answer.
- **Stop when you're done.** The most common failure is a good 60-second answer followed by 40 seconds of filler because the timer was still running. Finish and sit there. Dead air at the end costs you nothing.

## Tomorrow

Record it in the morning, not at 11pm the night it's due. You'll be sharper and you'll have room if the platform gives you trouble.

Want me to write out the "tell me about yourself" bullets properly, or throw you a few curveball questions to do a cold run against?
