First, you'd be using the same story three times: strength, weakness, and now this. In a one-way interview they watch your answers back to back and that's very noticeable.

Second, and bigger: this question has four beats and the pipeline version is missing the most important one. **"How did you inform others?"** That's what they're actually screening for. Everyone misses a goal at some point. What a manager wants to know is whether you say something early or go quiet and hope. So I need one thing from you.

## The question

**Was there a point this summer where you told Daniel, or whoever you reported to, that something was going to take longer or wasn't going to hit?** Could be the pipeline build. Could be anything else at GSDSI.

If yes, that's your story and it's a strong one, because it's the same project framed completely differently: the weakness answer is about why it slipped, this one is about what you did when you saw it slipping.

- You own it. No "the other person dropped the ball"
- You caught it **before** the deadline, not after
- You told someone directly rather than hoping to catch up quietly

### Claude · 2026-10-15 22:17

> *Claude used mcp__claude_ai__conversation_search: Ben Jones interview questions behavioral answers GSDSI internal tool*

> *Claude used mcp__claude_ai__conversation_search: MS-ISOM statement of purpose GSDSI expense tracker client data intake form*

Yes, and it's the strongest story you have for this question. I found it in your career readiness assessment from July, where you'd already written it out yourself. All four beats are there, which is rare.

Here's what you wrote then:

> "The limiting factor in each of these cases was not the software itself, but the lack of a process for rolling out the software: no one to own the software going forward, training, or documentation for the individuals who did not have me sitting beside them."

That's the whole answer. Use it.

---

**SITUATION**
- One of my internship goals was to build internal tools the company would actually use
- Built five: expense tracker, operations dashboard, presale analyzer, client data intake form, and a tools hub tying them together. Lovable, Supabase, GitHub
- Company is around seven people, everyone wearing several hats with priorities shifting daily

**WHEN I REALIZED**
- The tools hub went live and got early use, but **the spend hub and operations dashboard stalled in beta and the presale analyzer never rolled out company-wide**
- That's when it clicked that building the tool was maybe half the job
- **The limiting factor wasn't the software. It was that there was no rollout process.** No owner going forward, no training, no documentation for anyone who didn't have me sitting next to them
- I also realized I'd built some features that weren't a priority anymore by the time they shipped, because nobody had written down the scope up front

**HOW I INFORMED OTHERS**
- Didn't wait to be asked about it. Brought it to Daniel directly and named adoption as the problem, not the build
- **Wrote a brief for every tool** and presented it to Daniel and to the team that would actually use it, to get feedback
- Went to each team individually to learn how they actually work before changing anything

**RESULT**
- Leadership, operations and sales all got onboarded
- Adjusted permissions in the hub per team based on what I learned from each one, then did follow-up visits to make sure it stuck
- **Honest version: adoption depth still varies across the three teams, and sustaining it is the work that's left**
- What I'd do differently: a one-page scope doc agreed on before I build anything. That alone would have prevented most of the rework

---

Spoken flow:

> One of my goals this summer was to build internal tools the company would actually use. I built five of them, an expense tracker, an operations dashboard, a presale analyzer, a client data intake form, and a hub that tied them all together.
>
> The tools hub went live and got used. But the spend hub and the operations dashboard stalled out in beta, and the presale analyzer never rolled out company-wide. That's when I realized building the tool was maybe half the job. The limiting factor wasn't the software, it was that there was no rollout process behind it. Nobody owned it going forward, there was no training, and there was no documentation for anyone who didn't have me sitting next to them.
>
> I brought that to Daniel directly rather than waiting for someone to notice. I told him the problem was adoption, not the build. Then I wrote a brief for every tool and presented each one to him and to the team that would actually use it, and I went to each team individually to learn how they really worked before I changed anything.
>
> We got leadership, operations and sales onboarded, I adjusted permissions per team based on what I learned, and I did follow-up visits to make sure it stuck. I'll be honest that adoption depth still varies between those three teams and sustaining it is the work that's left. The thing I'd change is writing a one-page scope doc agreed on before I build anything. Most of the rework came from not having that.

---

Why this is the right one: it's a real miss, you own it completely, the "informing others" beat is genuine, the result is honest rather than a tidy win, and it ends on something concrete you'd change. That last honest note about adoption still varying is the strongest part. Most people wrap these up too neatly and it reads as fake.

Two small corrections while I'm here. GSDSI is about **seven** people, not nine, which I had wrong in the "why marketing" draft. And your internship title in these documents is **Marketing and Data Operations Apprentice**, while your resume says **Intern**. Pick one and use it everywhere.

That's it. Don't elaborate, don't explain your status, don't fill the remaining time. It's a compliance checkbox they're required to ask, and a long answer to a yes/no question looks worse than silence.

If the recording keeps running after you've answered, just sit there. Dead air on a 30-second question with a 5-second answer costs you nothing.

One thing to be sure about before you record: this posting says eligible applicants "must be legally authorized to work in the US and not require visa sponsorship now or in the future (including beyond OPT/EAD)." So answer it accurately. If your real answer is yes, you're not eligible for this req and it's better to know that now than after an offer. Answering "no" when the truth is "yes" is the kind of thing that gets discovered in the background check and ends the process permanently.

Checked Lenovo's actual job board. Here's the real picture, including one thing that will decide everything.

## The problem first

**LASR is the obvious fit and you're excluded.** The Lenovo Accelerated Sales Rotational Program Intern, Summer 2027 is exactly your profile: Morrisville NC, placements in Sales Support, Marketing, Customer Experience and Technical Client Advisory, prefers Business Analytics/Marketing/Sales majors with a 3.2+ GPA. You have a 3.8.

But it requires an **anticipated graduation date between May 2027 and May 2028.** You're May 2029. Same wall as Gartner.

## The one that's live and doesn't exclude you

**Global Supply Chain Intern, Summer 2027** — Morrisville, NC, req WD00105025, posted September 8.

Ignore the title. It has two tracks, and the second one is **Business Intelligence / Smart Ops: data visualization, automation and tool creation, data management and governance.** That is almost a description of what you did at GSDSI. You built dashboards, you automated a workflow nobody asked you to automate, you handled data hygiene.

Majors listed include Business, Statistics and Data Science. And critically, **the posting states no graduation date requirement.** It also feeds into Lenovo's full-time rotational program, which matters for your return-offer priority.

This is the one to apply to now.

## Two more that would be a better fit if they post

Lenovo runs six US intern tracks and posts them as separate reqs rather than one program. Two you'd enjoy more than supply chain:

- **Marketing & Communications** (Morrisville, or Chicago for Motorola Mobility)
- **Product Management / Data Analytics** (Morrisville)

Both were expected to post September through October 2026. I couldn't confirm either is live right now, so check the board yourself: **jobs.lenovo.com**, search "Summer 2027." Lenovo says it finishes much of its hiring by December, so these are worth checking weekly.

Also sitting on the board with no season attached: **Knowledge Management Data Analyst Intern**, Morrisville, req WD00105145. Couldn't load the page. Worth a look.

## What to actually ask your Lenovo contact

This is where a referral earns its keep. Grad-year filters are usually set by recruiting, not by the hiring manager, and a manager who wants you can often get an exception. Ask him directly:

> I found the LASR Summer 2027 posting and it's exactly what I want to do, but it asks for a May 2027 to May 2028 graduation date and I'm May 2029. Is that a hard filter or is there room for a strong sophomore? If it's hard, I'm also looking at the Global Supply Chain intern role for the Business Intelligence and Smart Ops track, which doesn't list a grad date and is closer to what I actually built this summer. Which would you point me toward?

That does three things: shows you read the postings, gives him an easy way to help, and gives him a fallback so he doesn't have to say no.

## Two things to weigh before you go hard at this

**Morrisville, North Carolina.** Lenovo's North America HQ, hybrid 3:2, and you'd have to live there for the full internship. You ranked pay relative to location cost as your number one factor, and this means a summer sublet in the Raleigh-Durham area. Raymond James in St. Pete at $20-25/hour with no housing cost may genuinely net you more money than Lenovo at a higher rate.

**Pay.** Lenovo doesn't post it for these roles, just "competitive compensation." For reference, their AI Research Engineer intern listing shows $32-46/hour and levels.fyi has Morrisville software interns at $29-43. Business and supply chain roles will be below that. Lenovo also states interns get no benefits beyond what's legally required.

## Timeline for LASR, if the exception works

Posted September 1, applications accepted August through January, Harver assessment and video interview ongoing, interview event February 2027, offers March 2027, program runs **May 17 to July 23, 2027**.

Note that program ends July 23 and Raymond James runs June 2 to August 13. They overlap completely, so this is an either-or, not a both.

Sources: [LASR Intern Summer 2027](https://jobs.lenovo.com/en_US/careers/JobDetail/Lenovo-Accelerated-Sales-Rotational-Program-Intern-LASR-Summer-2027/81187) · [Global Supply Chain Intern Summer 2027](https://jobs.lenovo.com/en_US/careers/JobDetail/Global-Supply-Chain-Intern-Summer-2027/81433) · [Lenovo internship tracks and timing](https://www.extern.com/post/lenovo-internship-guide)

### Claude · 2026-10-19 18:15

**What company/role was the interview based on?**

Raymond James, 2027 Summer Associate, Marketing Data Insights, based in St. Petersburg, FL.

**Questions Asked (3-5)**

Why Raymond James? Why this role and why marketing? What are your strengths and weaknesses? Tell me about a time you had a conflict with a teammate. Tell me about a time you worked with numerous deadlines, meetings, and appointments.

**What went well in your responses? What areas need improvement?**

What went well was my examples. I had specific work to point to, like the automated email campaign pipeline I built at my internship that took campaign launch from a full day to under ten minutes, and my mentor said the stories themselves were strong. The area that needed the most work was my "why Raymond James" answer. My mentor told me it came across as vague because I kept pointing back to their mission statement without explaining what actually made the firm different from its competitors, and he also pointed out that I was going all over the place in longer answers instead of landing on one clear point.

**Based on the mentor's feedback, what specific steps will you take to improve before your next interview?**

I rewrote my "why Raymond James" answer to name the specific thing that makes them stand out, which is that they lead with people rather than products, and to connect that to how their B2B marketing reaches their advisors. My mentor also told me to show I actually understand marketing by talking about it as a continuous cycle of testing and iterating from cold email through to a signed contract, so I worked that in. Most importantly, he said the biggest factor is not what you say but how you say it, so I am practicing each answer out loud and recording myself, keeping every response to 60 to 90 seconds with one concrete number in it.

- **Health:** [FILL — current training focus]. Running and badminton are the

## Top 3 goals right now

1. **Land a Summer 2027 internship.** Raymond James is the live one. Keep Adam's
   referral pipeline warm and keep applying to roles that don't gate on
   graduation year.
2. **Get Volvera to first paid revenue.** Systems are built. The gap is closing.
3. **Get actually good at SQL and Tableau** before June. The real bar from
   Raymond James' own analyst posting: joins, aggregations (COUNT, SUM, AVG,
   MIN, MAX, ORDER BY), and building interactive Tableau dashboards with filters.
   This is the one real gap between me and the roles I want.

## Standing context you should know

- **My binding constraint is my graduation date, not my resume.** Most Summer
  2027 postings require graduating by May 2028. I'm May 2029. Check the
  graduation-date line on any posting before anything else.
- **Geography:** Florida and East Coast hubs, or remote. Florida near home is
  worth more than a higher rate elsewhere once summer housing is priced in.
- **What I weigh:** pay relative to location cost first, then return-offer
  potential, then whether I'd actually be in the systems rather than making decks.
- **My stack:** Claude, n8n, Supabase, Lovable, GitHub, Instantly, Apple Calendar
  (everything lives there, including work blocks, not just meetings).

## How I like answers

**Direct.** Lead with the answer. No preamble, no restating my question back
to me, no "great question."

**Plain English.** If I'd have to read a sentence twice, it's wrong. No
"leverage," "utilize," "streamline," "thrive in ambiguity." No em dashes.
I will tell you when something sounds like AI slop, and when I do, rewrite it
in actual human words instead of defending it.

**Short unless the task is big.** A yes/no question gets a yes or no.

**Don't invent facts about me.** If you need a detail you don't have, ask.
Making up a project, a number, or a story I didn't do is worse than useless
because I might repeat it in an interview.

**Flag my inconsistencies.** If a number on my resume doesn't match what I said
out loud, or a title is different across two documents, tell me. That has caught
real problems.

**Bullets when I'm going to speak it, prose when I'm going to send it.** I don't
read off scripts. Give me the beats I can't forget, not a paragraph to memorize.

**Copy-paste ready.** If it's going into a doc, form, or email, format it so I
can lift it directly.

**Tell me when I'm wrong.** Including when I don't want to hear it, and including
when what I'm asking for would hurt me.
